S213 to S222: chunk 31, 2026.10.24

The record: _handoffs/math98_chunk31_2026.10.24/README.md.

S213. Logic binds below the relations: or at 10, and at 20, not at 30, the relations at 40, the arithmetic as before. G5's table listed the relations loosest, above or, and gave x > 0 and x < 1 as its example, which that order reads as x > (0 and x) < 1. C, Python and Mathematica put and and or below the relations, and Python and Mathematica not too, so that not x = y is not (x = y). The roadmap's table now has the order, with a note.

  • Alternative: G5's table as written, its example then needing brackets, (x > 0) and (x < 1).

Review. Taken: for, the order C, Python and Mathematica share; against, a departure from G5's table as written. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S214. A chain of relations is the conjunction of its links: a < b <= c is a < b and b <= c, a = b = c is a = b and b = c, and a < b > c is a < b and b > c. As in mathematics and in Python. The relations are non-associative in the table, the climber groups a chain to the right, and the parser layer joins its links after the climb, left to right, in one pass down the chain. An operand between two links stands in both, so that the term holds it twice (section 6).

  • Alternatives: a chain refused, as chunk 27 thought a parser might, so that a < b < c is an error; or C's reading, (a < b) <= c, a boolean compared with a number, which a value calls a domain error.

Review. Taken: for, mathematics' and Python's chains; against, the shared operand appears twice in the term, so it is folded twice and the term is larger. Recommended: as taken in meaning, the operand shared in the representation later. Taken.

S215. A pair of round brackets is read as one operand before the climb, as calls and bars are (S209). The climber then meets no bracket, so that a relation it makes an operand of another is a link of the same chain, and a bracketed relation, (a < b) < c, is one operand and no link. The term keeps no trace of the brackets, as before.

  • Alternative: a node for brackets in the term, which every fold and rewrite would have to step through; or the climber left the brackets and the chains told by the tokens' places.

Review. Taken: for, a bracketed relation is no link, and folds see no bracket nodes; against, the author's redundant brackets are not kept in the text. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S216. piecewise is read in math/function/function.hpp's vocabulary: piecewise(when(c1, v1), ..., otherwise(d)) is the call piecewise(c1, v1, ..., d), and the renderer writes it back in its branches. The term stays one call of the function table, its arguments the conditions and values in turn, so the folds need nothing new; when and otherwise stand only as whole arguments of piecewise, an otherwise only last, and each misuse is an error at its place.

  • Alternatives: when and otherwise as functions of their own, whose values would be pairs, a kind the language has not; or an operator kind of its own, which expr_op_kind has not.

Review. Taken: for, one call, no new kind, written back in its branches; against, none. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S217. As a value a relation is a boolean: of two numbers by value.h's exact order, across kinds, none holding of a NaN but !=; of two booleans = and != alone; an error passes, the first; anything else is a domain error. Logic is Kleene's: a false operand makes and false and a true one makes or true whatever the others are; otherwise an error passes, the first, and what is no boolean is a domain error. A boolean carries no flags. Exact where possible (0.1 + 0.2 = 0.3 holds), and an error is what value.h passes. Kleene's logic is the one in which an unknown operand that cannot change the answer does not: false and sqrt(-1) > 0 is false. value.h's comparison gives an order, and no flags, so the boolean has none.

  • Alternatives: booleans ordered as value.h orders them, false below true; a number's truth as C's in logic, nonzero true; strict logic, in which an error anywhere passes.

Review. Taken: for, relations exact across kinds, so 0.1 + 0.2 = 0.3 holds, and Kleene's logic, the one SQL and three-valued logics share, in which an unknown operand that cannot change the answer does not; against, a comparison of a NaN is false, not unknown, as IEEE 754's is. The alternatives: C's truth of a number in logic hides a slip, and strict logic lets an error that cannot matter spoil the answer. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S218. In a type the booleans are 1 and 0: a relation is C's comparison, which holds of no NaN but !=; logic reads 0 as false, a NaN as unknown, as an error is as a value, and any other number as true; a boolean bound to a variable, or a number a fold made, is 1 or 0 where 2b made it a NaN. The typed folds are C's arithmetic, and the booleans 1 and 0 are C's; a NaN is a type's error.

  • Alternative: a relation of a NaN a NaN in a type, unknown, which no C comparison gives.

Review. Taken: for, the typed folds stay C's arithmetic, and the booleans 1 and 0 are what C gives; against, a relation of a NaN is 0 in a type where its logic reads a NaN as unknown. The alternative, a NaN for such a relation, is what no C comparison gives. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S219. true and false are constants of the table: 1 and 0 in each type, the booleans as values. A fold's boolean is written true or false, as render.hpp has written it since 2b, and now reads back as the constant, whose value it is.

  • Alternative: no names, the booleans only the relations' values.

Review. Taken: for, a fold's boolean reads back as itself; against, two names taken from the variables. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S220. all_of and any_of, from the vocabulary, are and and or of any number of arguments from 1, and piecewise takes any number from 1; a function of any number of arguments says so in its arity's error, "all_of takes at least 1 argument, not 0". The vocabulary's own names.

  • Alternative: all_of() true and any_of() false, the empty conjunction and disjunction: a text with no operand is more likely a slip.

Review. Taken: for, an empty call is more likely a slip than meant; against, all_of() and any_of() have values, true and false, the empty conjunction's and disjunction's, which Python's all and any, Mathematica's And[] and Or[] and SymPy's And() and Or() give, and which a call over a list made by a program, a vector's elements once 3b can spread them, will meet. Recommended: the alternative, the identities, with piecewise kept at one argument at least. Ruled 2026.11.04: the alternative, as recommended, at any step that touches the folds' logic. Flag: not the recommendation; a minor point, changeable later, as it makes an error a value.

S221. The face test grows in place: the not_yet checks of the relations and logic become checks of what they do; a boolean bound in a type is 1 or 0; the relations, logic, true, false, piecewise and all_of join the random round trip. As S211.

  • Alternative: a test of 2d's own beside it.

Review. Taken: as S211. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S222. The checks: as S212, against chunk 30's delivered tree, with 424 planted faults; the depth probe folds to a value too, with two shapes of 2d's.

  • Alternative: the full tier again, of which only the new test's 64 builds can tell this change from chunk 30's.

S223 to S227: chunk 32, 2026.10.24

S223. Each member of the handle delegates to its term-level entry, and answers as the entry does now: subs and simplify give the term unchanged until 2f, as expr_substitute and expr_simplify do, and diff a handle of expr_derivative's not_yet until 4a. 2a's skeleton said the members would ("each answers as they do once it is filled"); so 2f and 4a fill the members when they fill the entries, and change nothing in expr.hpp.

S225. An error passes through every member unchanged. Of subs's two, the handle's own comes out; the replacement's comes out whether or not its variable occurs in the term. S228 to S236: chunk 33, 2026.10.24

The record: _handoffs/math98_chunk33_2026.10.24/README.md.

S228. Simplification keeps the value at every binding at which each part of the term has a finite value, a number or a boolean: exactly where the term's value is exact, and the same real number where it is not. Where a part's value is an error, an infinity or a NaN, the simplified term's may differ; a part drops only where it is well formed. The roadmap's own rules ask for it: 0 + x is x, and like terms collected make x - x 0, which is no longer the value where x is infinite or an error. A simplifier that kept each binding's value, its errors and infinities among them, could drop nothing that might be infinite, 0 * y keeping its y. Computer algebra systems commonly make x - x 0 in the same way. Being well formed keeps the errors no binding takes away, an unknown name's or a wrong arity's, which mark a mistake in the text, not a point outside the domain.

  • Alternative: each binding's value kept, its errors and infinities included: x - x and 0 * x as they are, until the variables can be known finite, of which Phase 2 has no way to say.

Review. Taken: for, the rules every computer algebra system applies, x - x 0 and 0 * y 0, kept honest by a stated guarantee, the value kept wherever each part is finite, and x / x kept, as it is undefined at 0; against, x - x is 0 where x is infinite, where its value is a NaN, and nothing in the result says so. The alternative keeps every value but simplifies almost nothing. Recommended: as taken now; once the language can state assumptions (x finite, x nonzero), simplification under them, and an entry that gives the conditions a rewrite took, as Mathematica's ConditionalExpression and SymPy's assumptions do. Taken.

S229. A node folds only where each operand is an exact number, true or false, and only to an exact value or a boolean, the boolean written as the constant true or false; an inexact value, or an error, leaves the node as it is. An exact fold changes nothing a later step can tell: 1/3 + 1/6 and 1/2 are one number, in every type and as a value. A rounded one would: sqrt(2), folded to the long double nearest it, is the square root of 2 no longer, to the derivative or to a fold in another type; and a relation of inexact operands, pi < 4, answers by a rounding. The constants keep the language's text: 1 < 2 is true, which reads back as itself.

  • Alternative: every closed node folded to its value, rounded where it is inexact, as a numeric simplifier would; or a node of any closed operands, pi among them, folded where its value comes out exact or a boolean.

Review. Taken: for, no fold rounds, so sqrt(2) stays the square root of 2 for the derivative and for a fold in any type; against, sqrt(2) * sqrt(2) stays as written, as no rule of radicals makes it 2. The alternative would make simplification depend on a rounding. Recommended: as taken, with exact rules of radicals and powers later (Phase 4's simplification). Taken.

S230. Like terms are terms with the same other factors, in any order, each dividing as it does, compared by parse/expression's structural equality; a coefficient is an exact number, and division by an exact number other than 0 goes into it. A collected sum keeps the order in which each group's first term stands, and writes a coefficient first. So x * y + y * x is 2 * x * y and x / 2 + x / 2 is x, and a term keeps the order its author wrote: x + 1 + x is 2 * x + 1, where a canonical order would make 1 + x into x + 1.

  • Alternative: like terms only with their factors in the same order, x * y and y * x two terms; or a canonical order of terms and factors, sorted, which would also find sin(x * y) and sin(y * x) alike (section 6), but rewrites every term, one with nothing to collect among them.

Review. Taken: for, x * y + y * x is 2 * x * y, and the author's order is kept; against, two drawbacks, the first mended in 3b. Factors were taken to commute, so a matrix product was collected as if it did: in chunk 34 [1, 2] * [3; 4] - [3; 4] * [1, 2] simplified to 0 * [1, 2] * [3; 4] and A * B + B * A of two literals to 2 * A * B; from 3b a factor that holds a matrix keeps its place (S251). And like terms are found by structure alone, so sin(x * y) and sin(y * x) are two terms. Recommended: a canonical comparison key, built for the comparison and never written into the term, so the author's order is kept and sin(x * y) and sin(y * x) are alike; and factors commuting only where each is a number, as 3b made them (S251), matrix variables declared as S251's review asks. Taken, its drawback from 3b mended by S251; the key a minor point.

S231. A rule applies only where it makes the node smaller, by parse/expression's size, or a sum as small with fewer terms. Simplification then never makes a term larger, a pass that changes the term makes it smaller or a sum in it shorter, and a term with nothing to simplify comes back as it was: x * 2 as x * 2, and a - (b - c) as written.

  • Alternative: each sum and product written in its collected form, whatever its size: x * 2 as 2 * x, and a - (b - c) as a - b + c.

Review. Taken: for, simplification never makes a term larger and keeps what has nothing to simplify as written; against, no canonical form, so two equal terms may simplify to different texts. Recommended: as taken for simplify, with expand, factor and a canonical form as entries of their own, as SymPy's expand, factor and together are. Taken.

S232. Simplification makes passes of its own over the term, bottom up, collecting each sum and product once a pass, at the root of its chain, until a pass changes nothing, and at most 1,024 passes, as many as rewrite_to_fixpoint makes; not rewrite_to_fixpoint itself, with the rules its rewrite at every node. A chain's terms are collected together, where a rewrite at a node sees that node: collected at each node, a chain of n terms would be collected below each of its n nodes, n times over, where once at its root does it.

  • Alternative: rewrite_to_fixpoint, each node's chain collected where the node stands.

Review. Taken: for, a chain collected once at its root, not once below each of its nodes; against, a pass of its own beside rewrite_to_fixpoint. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S233. Substitution puts a term in place of a variable alone: a named constant and a function's name stay. The replacement goes in whole, and is not searched again. Substitution in a term made by default gives it back, and such a term put in place of a variable the term holds makes the result one. The folds' bindings bind variables alone, and substitution binds a variable to a term; one fold over the term keeps x by x + 1 from going on without end. A term made by default is the language's no-term: in it there is no node to replace, and put in, it leaves none where the variable stood.

  • Alternative: a named constant replaced too, pi by 3 making pi * f(x) into 3 * f(x); and a term made by default, put in, leaving the term as it was.

Review. Taken: for, substitution binds what the folds' bindings bind, and one pass cannot run on without end; against, a named constant cannot be replaced, which a binding of its name does. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S234. The factorial's loops in function.hpp, in a type and exactly, end at the type's largest binary exponent at the latest, as well as where the product passes the type's range. n! is past 2^n from n = 4, and so past the range by then: where std::isinf answers truly, the loop has ended before, and no answer changes. Where it does not, as under valgrind, each loop ran to n, and the 18 large factorials 2f's random checks ask for kept valgrind on the test past the queue's two hours (section 2.5). The change is in 2c's code, outside 2f's own: it changes no answer, and without it the test cannot be run under valgrind.

  • Alternative: function.hpp as it was, and the test no longer run under valgrind; or 2f's random checks kept from large factorials, which the round trip's alphabet, its literal 123456789 and the factorial, makes.

Review. Taken: for, no answer changes and the test runs under valgrind; against, a change in 2c's code made in 2f's chunk. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S235. The face test grows in place: the rewrites' part keeps the derivative's 3 stubs, and eight new parts check substitution and simplification, two of them on random terms, of 2d's language and of an alphabet of arithmetic beside it. As S226.

  • Alternative: a test of the rewrites' own beside it.

Review. Taken: as S226. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S236. The checks: as S227, against chunk 32's delivered tree, with 500 planted faults. The fuzz simplifies each random term, no larger, a fixed point and read back the same, its value kept at an exact binding where each part is finite, and substitutes a random term in each, as a value and in double; the depth probe simplifies each shape too; the comparison with chunk 32 reads chunk 32's fuzz's texts by both trees and must find every line the same.

  • Alternative: the full tier again, of which only the new test's 64 builds can tell this change from chunk 32's.

Review. Taken: as S227. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S237 to S245: chunk 34, 2026.10.25

The record: _handoffs/math98_chunk34_2026.10.25/README.md.

S237 to S245, chunk 34's, as decisions.html has them; each is reviewed there (section 2.6), the 18 flagged decisions of S1 to S245 listed first.

S237. The matrix kind's table is written in C++, in math/expr/matrix.hpp, from C++11, as the language that makes and reads matrices is; not in C, as value_wide.h's wide integer is. Its elements are math::values, which hold their boxes as C++ objects do, and its storage is std::vectors; value.h meets it through the table alone, unchanged. A kind in C would serve C callers, who have no expression language to make a matrix with, and would hold its elements' boxes by hand.

  • Alternative: the table in C, c/math/value_matrix.h beside value_wide.h, with a C harness of its own, and math/expr/ reading it through the C functions.

Review. Taken: for, elements held as C++ objects hold their boxes, the table sits beside the one language that makes matrices, and the owner's principle 1 gives element types other than the three floating-point ones a generic C++ path; against, a C program has no matrix value, and the risk that 3b and 3c write matrix arithmetic again in matrix.hpp. The alternative, the kind in C beside value_wide.h, would serve C callers who have no language to make a matrix with, at the cost of holding every element's box by hand. Recommended: as taken, with 3b's and 3c's arithmetic not written in matrix.hpp but delegated, a dense matrix's to the C core's mat.h and vec.h, whose row-major arrays its storage already is, and a matrix of values to linear_algebra's generic matrices of math::value (chunk 26, from C++98), so each algorithm is written once. Taken; the delegation done by 3b's arithmetic (S247) and 3c's functions (S257), the C core's decompositions beside the kernels.

S238. One kind, "matrix", of rank 1000, past every number's: the long double's 500 and the wide integer's 210, with room left below it for bignum's and the complex numbers'. A matrix is no number: it has no trait, converts to no number, and value.h hands every operation with a matrix among its operands to the matrix's table. Its storage, values or a dense array of one floating-point type, is the box's, not the kind's; a vector is a matrix.

  • Alternative: four kinds, an exact matrix and a dense one of each floating-point type, each of a rank of its own; or a vector kind beside the matrix.

Review. Taken: for, one table, the storage the box's; against, none. The alternative's four kinds would multiply the tables and their cross-kind cases. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S239. A matrix keeps each element as it is given: as a dense array of float, double or long double where every element is one of those, in the widest one's type, to which each widens exactly; and as math::values, each of its own kind, where any element is exact or boxed. No element is rounded by being put in a matrix, so an exact matrix stays exact for 3c's determinant and inverse, an exact element beside an inexact one stays exact until an operation of 3b combines them, and the dense array, what Eigen and BLAS take, is what a matrix of floating-point numbers is. (Taken first, before this chunk's delivery, as the tower's storage: dense in the widest inexact element's type wherever one element was inexact, each exact element its nearest there. That rounded an exact 1/3 beside a double to its nearest double on making the matrix, before any operation asked for it; it was changed in this chunk, and the faults that test the storage were planted again on the new passage.)

  • Alternative: the tower's storage, as first taken; always values, a dense array only on request; or a dense array wherever the elements fit one, exact integers among them.

Review. Taken, revised in this chunk: for, no element is rounded by being put in a matrix, an exact matrix stays exact for 3c, and a matrix of floating-point numbers is the dense array Eigen and BLAS take; against, a matrix of one exact element among thousands of doubles is held as values, slower than dense, until an operation combines them. The rule first taken, the tower's storage, rounded an exact element on making the matrix, before any operation asked for it. Recommended: as taken now. Taken.

S240. A vector is a matrix of one row: [1, 2, 3] is 1 by 3, and a column is written [1; 2; 3], as MATLAB writes them; expr_vector makes the one row. Every shape then has a literal that reads back as itself: [a] is 1 by 1, [a, b] 1 by 2, [a; b] 2 by 1.

  • Alternative: a vector a column, n by 1, the shape A v takes in a textbook; a matrix of one row would then have no literal until 3b's transpose, [1, 2, 3]'.

Review. Taken: for, every shape has a literal that reads back as itself, as MATLAB writes them; against, SymPy's Matrix([1, 2, 3]), Eigen's VectorXd and Julia's [1, 2, 3] are columns, as textbooks write A v, so A * [1, 2] will be a shape error where a reader of those expects a product. The alternative leaves a row with no literal before 3b's transpose. Recommended: as taken, documented with the column [1; 2] beside it. Taken.

S241. An element is a number: an element that is an error passes, the first, and one that is no number, a boolean, none or a matrix, makes the matrix a domain error, as in arithmetic; so [[1, 2], 3] is a domain error, not a concatenation. A matrix has a row and a column at least: [] is a missing operand, as an empty element or row is. A matrix literal's rows have as many elements each as its first, or the literal is bad_shape, at the semicolon or the bracket that ends the row of another number. The place keeps the parse's errors met left to right, as each element's own error stands before the end of its row.

  • Alternative: a nested literal concatenated, as MATLAB's [[1, 2], 3] is [1, 2, 3]; an empty matrix, 0 by 0; the shape's error at the row's first element.

Review. Taken: for, an element is a number, as in arithmetic, and a ragged row's error stands at its place; against, no empty matrix, which 3c's kernels and null spaces give (an n by 0 basis), and no block literal, [A, B; C, D] of matrices of fitting shapes, which MATLAB, Julia and Eigen's comma initializer have. Recommended: as taken for 3a; empty matrices and block literals by 3c, both of which make an error a value and so change no literal that reads now. Taken in full with chunk 36: the empty matrix (S260) and blocks (S261), a literal of numbers alone read and refused as before.

S242. Until step 20 (3b) a matrix computes nothing. In a type a literal has no value, bad_shape, an operand's error coming first, as at any node. As a value, arithmetic, relations, logic and functions on a matrix are a domain error, by the kind's table and by function.hpp's guard. The kind's comparison finds two matrices equal where they are of one shape and their elements are equal, place by place, by value.h's order, whatever their storage, and finds no order otherwise. Two atoms of one matrix are then one atom, as structural equality, and simplification's fixed point, ask. A variable bound to a matrix is a NaN in a type, as one bound to none is.

  • Alternative: not_yet for each operation on a matrix, naming step 20; or the sums and the scalar products now, ahead of their step.

Review. Taken: for, nothing computes before its step, and the comparison two atoms of one matrix need is there; against, none. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S243. Indexing waits for step 20 (3b): a [ after an operand's end is indexing, v[i], and answers not_yet, naming step 20, and is never an implied product, with implied multiplication on too: 2[1] is not 2 * [1]. The roadmap's 3a names the kinds and the literals alone; operator.hpp's note had placed index in 3a, and now places it in 3b, the step of the other postfix operator, the transpose, whose roadmap item now names indexing as well.

  • Alternative: v[i] and A[i, j] now, by expr_element, counted from 1 as in mathematics, or from 0 as in C.

Review. Taken: for, 2[1] is never a silent product, and indexing waits for its step; against, none. Recommended: as taken; in 3b, indices counted from 1 in the language, as mathematics, MATLAB, Julia and Mathematica count, and from 0 in the C++ functions, as expr_element counts. Taken.

S244. The face test grows in place: section 11 checks the kind and the entries, and four new parts the literals in the parser, in the renderer and in the folds, and on 1,000 random literals drawn by a generator of their own, so that the random parts before them draw what they drew. Chunk 33's two checks that took a parse's value without asking whether it held one now ask (chunk 33's finding), and the new checks read every result, every shape and every box through helpers that give none, or nothing, where a fault leaves none. A fault then fails checks rather than read what is not there; one stops the test by a signal only where the code it faults does.

  • Alternative: a test of the literals' own beside the face test; or the literals in the round trip's generator, which would change what every random part after it draws.

Review. Taken: for, every random part before the literals draws what it drew, and a fault fails checks rather than read what is not there; against, none. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S245. The checks: as S236, against chunk 33's delivered tree, with 569 planted faults: chunk 33's 500, 18 of them moved with the passages step 19 wrote again, F199 put in anew, and 69 in step 19's code, run in three parts. The fuzz as chunk 33's, its random texts now spelling literals; the depth probe with a third sweep, of nested literals; and the comparison with chunk 33's random texts, every line the same but those chunk 33 answered with step 19's not_yet, each of which must read otherwise now.

  • Alternative: the full tier again, of which only the face test's 64 builds can tell this change from chunk 33's.

Review. Taken: as S236. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S246 to S255: chunk 35, 2026.10.26

The record: _handoffs/math98_chunk35_2026.10.26/README.md.

S246 to S255, chunk 35's, as decisions.html has them; each is reviewed there (section 2.5), the 20 flagged decisions of S1 to S255 listed first.

S246. The matrix kind computes what linear algebra defines, and nothing else: A + B and A - B of one shape, element by element; -A; A * s, s * A and A / s, of a matrix and a number, each element by the number; and A * B, A's columns as many as B's rows, the matrix product. Every other operation on a matrix is a domain error: A + s, s + A, A - s and s - A; s / A and A / B; |A|; and two matrices of shapes the operation does not take. A 1 by 1 matrix stays a matrix: [1, 2] * [3; 4] is [11], not 11. Each of these has one meaning in every text on linear algebra; a matrix plus a number has two (each element plus the number, as MATLAB and NumPy broadcast it, or the number times the identity, as some texts write it), and a quotient of matrices is A times B's inverse on one side or the other, which 3c's inv and solve will say outright. An error of shapes is then an error at its place, never a broadcast that hides it.

  • Alternative: MATLAB's and NumPy's broadcasting, A + s each element plus s and A / B as A * inv(B); or a 1 by 1 result taken for its number.

Review. Taken: for, every operation the kind computes has the one meaning linear algebra gives it, and an error of shapes is an error at its place, as SymPy's matrices and Eigen's give it, never a broadcast that hides a mistake; against, what MATLAB and NumPy users write by habit, A + 1 for each element plus 1, is an error here, and there is no element-wise product, quotient or power, A .* B, A ./ B, A .^ 2, which MATLAB, Julia and NumPy have. The alternative, broadcasting, would make A + s mean one thing where texts on linear algebra mean another, and A / B would be an inverse that 3c has not brought. Recommended: as taken, with the element-wise operators as operators of their own, spelled apart from the algebraic ones (MATLAB's and Julia's .*, ./, .^), when a step asks for them; A / B as 3c's solve. Taken.

S247. Each operation is computed by linear algebra's kernels, written once (linear_algebra/linalg_common.hpp): a dense matrix's by the C core's vec.h and mat.h, in the wider of its operands' types, each element widened exactly first, and an exact number converted to that type as the tower converts it, so that a matrix of floats times 2 stays floats; a matrix that holds values, or any operation with one, by the generic path, in math::value's own arithmetic, exact wherever its elements are. A result is stored as S239 stores any matrix: dense where every element is floating, as values otherwise. A dense matrix keeps no flags of its own elements, so each element read from it, by expr_element, by indexing or by the arithmetic of values, carries the matrix's flags: an inexact element (G2) is never read as an exact one's neighbour without its flag. Storage that cannot be had is D_MATH_VALUE_ERROR_MEMORY, as value.h asks of a kind's entries, and nothing throws. The kernels are those 3c's det, inv and solve will use, so the language and linear_algebra give the same bits for the same product. (The flags: chunk 34's expr_element read a dense element without them; it now gives them, as this step's indexing does.)

  • Alternative: the arithmetic written here, element by element, on math::values alone; or every operation on dense matrices in long double, whatever their type.

Review. Taken: for, each operation written once, in the kernels 3c and linear_algebra share, so a product has the same bits wherever it is computed, and an exact matrix stays exact through the generic path; against, a matrix of values and a dense one meet on the generic path, one element at a time, slower than the dense kernel, and a dense matrix's elements share one set of flags. The alternative of arithmetic written here would be a second implementation to keep in step; long double throughout would round a float matrix's products otherwise than the type does. Recommended: as taken; per-element flags are not needed while the one flag is G2's inexact, which every element computed from an inexact one carries anyway. Taken.

S248. A ^ n is the power of a square matrix to a whole n >= 0, exact or floating-point; to a floating-point n, the matrix is first taken to the wider of its type and n's, as the tower takes a number to a power of another kind. It is computed by repeated squaring, A's square, its square's, and so on, multiplied into the power where n's bits are 1, each product the kernels'. A ^ 0 is the identity of A's size in A's storage: exact for a matrix of values, in its type for a dense one, as a floating-point number to the power 0 is 1 in its type. Any other power is a domain error: a matrix not square, n negative (which needs A's inverse, 3c's), n not whole, infinite, a NaN or past the exact integers' range, and a number to a matrix's power; pow(A, n) is A ^ n. Squaring takes at most 2 log2(n) products where repeated multiplication takes n - 1, and a dense power's rounding grows with their number: the 90th power of [1, 1; 1, 0], exact, F(91) and F(90) among its elements, takes 9. The C core's d_mat_power multiplies A ^ (k - 1) by A, so a dense matrix's power here and there may differ in their last bits.

  • Alternative: the C core's d_mat_power, n - 1 products; an exact n alone, a floating-point 2 a domain error; A ^ 0 always exact.

Review. Taken: for, at most 2 log2(n) products where n - 1 would do, exact powers of exact matrices whatever their size, and a floating-point 2 taken as 2, the power in the wider type, as the tower takes a number to it; against, the C core's d_mat_power computes A ^ (k - 1) A, so a dense matrix's power differs in its last bits between the language and the C core's function, two answers for one operation where the framework's principle (4) wants one; and a negative power waits for 3c's inverse. The alternative, the C core's n - 1 products, would agree with it, at more products and more rounding. Recommended: as taken, with the C core's d_mat_power changed to repeated squaring, its own tests then checking the same powers, so both agree; and A ^ (-n) as inv(A) ^ n in 3c, for an invertible A. Taken in full with chunk 36: d_mat_power, the generic kernel's power and dynamic_matrix's square as the language does (S262), and A ^ (-n) is inv(A) ^ n (S259).

S249. Indexing counts from 1, as mathematics, MATLAB, Julia and Mathematica count: A[i, j] is the element in row i, column j; v[i] the i-th element of a vector, a matrix of one row or of one column; and A[i] of any other matrix its row i, a matrix of one row, the row Mathematica's A[[i]] and NumPy's A[i] give. An index is a whole number of any kind, exact or floating-point, from 1 to the places it counts; anything else is a domain error, as is indexing what is no matrix, and an error passes, the first, the matrix's before an index's. A[i, j] with i or j out of range is a domain error, not bad_shape, as the index is a value the fold computed. In a type, where no value is a matrix, indexing is a NaN, as a variable bound to a matrix is. A' of a number is the number, a number being its own transpose; of an error the error; of a boolean or none a domain error. The C++ functions count from 0 (expr_element), as C++ does (S243's review).

  • Alternative: indices from 0, as C and NumPy count; MATLAB's A(i), the i-th element down the columns, for any matrix; an exact index alone.

Review. Taken: for, the indices of mathematics and of every language of linear algebra but NumPy's, an index computed by any fold accepted where it is whole, and the errors of value.h's order; against, no slices, A[1:2, :], no index from the end, MATLAB's end, and no logical indexing, which MATLAB, Julia and NumPy have. The alternatives: indices from 0 would read against every text the language's users write from; MATLAB's linear A(i) gives a column-major element of a matrix whose storage is row by row, and an exact index alone would refuse the 2.0 a fold in double gives. Recommended: as taken, with slices and an end as later items, when a step asks for them; the C++ functions counting from 0, as expr_element does. Taken.

S250. The parser keeps each nesting's stack frame small: the work of a group, its end, its term, its errors and its rows, is done in functions of its own, kept out of line, outside the function that recurses into nested groups, so that each level of nesting costs that function's frame and the range's alone. Chunk 34 kept a literal's rows in every bracket's frame, and nested brackets stopped the program sooner than chunk 33's (chunk 34's finding); step 20, which rewrote that function for indexing, moved the work out, and nested brackets and calls now read as deep as chunk 33's did or deeper (section 6). The depth itself is still the owner's to bound (chunk 33's finding).

  • Alternative: the work inline, as chunk 34 had it; or an explicit stack of groups, no recursion at all, which would bound the depth by memory rather than by the stack.

Review. Taken: for, nested brackets and calls read as deep as chunk 33's or deeper on the same stack, and the depth no longer falls with each step that adds to a group; against, the depth is still the stack's, and a deep enough text still ends the program (chunk 33's finding). The alternative, an explicit stack of groups, would read any depth memory holds; the folds, the renderer and simplification recurse as deep as the term all the same, so a parser that read deeper would hand them terms they cannot fold. Recommended: as taken, with a depth guard over the whole module, as chunk 33's finding asks of the owner: an error, too_deep, past a bound the owner sets. Taken.

S251. Simplification keeps the order of every factor that holds a matrix, a literal or a matrix a fold made: two terms are alike only where those factors stand in the same order, so that A * B + B * A stays and A * B + A * B is 2 * A * B, while numbers, variables and any other factor commute with everything, so that 2 * A * x + x * A is 3 * A * x. No rule makes an error of matrices a value: A ^ 1 is A only where A is square by its shape, a literal or a matrix a fold made; in a sum that holds a matrix the terms of coefficient 0 it drops are written as one 0, since a matrix plus a number is an error (S246): [1, 2] + 0 stays, and x - x + [1, 2] is 0 + [1, 2]. A literal, or a matrix a fold made, is never well formed (S228), so 0 * A stays and [1, 2] - [1, 2] is 0 * [1, 2], the zero matrix. A variable is taken for a number, as a symbol is in SymPy until it is declared a matrix: bound to matrices, x * y + y * x is 2 * x * y all the same, and x ^ 0 is 1. This is the order S230's review (chunk 34) asked of step 20, before matrix products had values.

  • Alternative: every factor kept in its order, numbers among them, so that x * y + y * x stays; or variables taken for matrices, which commute with nothing.

Review. Taken: for, A * B + B * A stays and A * B + A * B collects, numbers and variables still commute, and no simplification turns an error of matrices into a value; against, a variable is taken for a number, so for variables bound to matrices A * B + B * A becomes 2 * A * B and A ^ 0 becomes 1, a different value, and the terms of variables a matrix user writes are those most often simplified. The alternatives: no factor commuting would leave x * y + y * x uncollected, every variable a matrix would collect nothing. Recommended: declared domains (S274), declared matrix variables among them, as SymPy's MatrixSymbol and its non-commutative symbols are: a binding's or the syntax's mark that a name is a matrix, of a shape where known, or a quaternion, which simplification then treats as a literal is treated now, its powers' identities included; in step 25 (4a), or sooner where a user simplifies terms of matrix variables. Ruled 2026.11.04: declared domains, matrix variables among them, at step 25 (4a); S376's variables beside quaternions with them. Flag: significant drawback for variables bound to matrices.

S252. Indexing goes through the shared climber: a [ after an operand's end opens the operand's indices, never an implied product (S243), read as one group, each index split at the group's commas and parsed in turn, and stands in the climb as a postfix operator of a table the parser layer adds to the standard one, at a precedence above every other, 100, so that -v[1] is -(v[1]), 2 ^ v[1] is 2 ^ (v[1]), A[1][2] indexes the row A[1] and x'[1] is (x')[1]; once the climb is done it is put back as the term index of its operand and its indices. The transpose, A', is the standard table's, postfix, between the unary minus and ^, at 75: A' ^ 2 is (A') ^ 2, A ^ 2' is (A ^ 2)', as MATLAB reads both, -A' is -(A'), and x!' is (x!)'. The renderer writes an index after its operand, bare where the operand is a leaf, a call, a literal, an index or a postfix operator's, and in round brackets otherwise, each reading back as itself. An index's operand is whatever stands before it, the climb's own operand, so that a postfix operator of the climber's is the one way to give it one; and A ^ 2' is the power's transpose wherever it is written, as a MATLAB user means it. G5's table, which put ' beside ! above ^, is revised so, as S213 revised it for logic (roadmap.html).

  • Alternative: the transpose at the factorial's place, 90, tighter than ^, so that A ^ 2' is A ^ (2'), A ^ 2 silently; indexing as a group of its own before the climb, as a call is, which cannot index a parenthesized term, (a + b)[1], or a transpose.

Review. Taken: for, indexing of any operand, a group's and a transpose's among them, through the shared climber as S190 kept it, and A ^ 2' read as MATLAB reads it, so that no MATLAB text means another thing silently; against, a table of the parser layer's own beside the standard one, a postfix operator whose indices the climber never sees, put back after it, and a transpose looser than the factorial, so that 2 ^ 3! is 2 ^ 6 while 2 ^ 3' is (2 ^ 3)'. The alternative transpose, at 90, would read A ^ 2' as A ^ 2 with no error; indexing as a group before the climb could not index (a + b) or A'. Recommended: as taken within S190's shared climber, whose own alternative, a precedence parser of the language's, would hold postfix operators with operands as it holds calls. Ruled 2026.11.04: decided on 2026.11.06 with lane F against expression core v2's design: core v2 if its parser holds postfix operators and calls, S209 and S252 then following it; otherwise the language's own precedence parser, as S190's review recommends. Flag: follows from S190.

S253. A relation of two matrices is = or !=: they are equal where they are of one shape and their elements are equal, place by place, by value.h's order, whatever their storage, as the kind's comparison finds them (S242). An order of matrices, A < B, and a relation of a matrix and a number, A = 1, are domain errors. So A' = A asks whether A is symmetric, and (A * B)' = B' * A' holds.

  • Alternative: = and != element by element, a matrix of booleans, as MATLAB's == gives; or an order of matrices, by their elements in turn.

Review. Taken: for, A' = A asks a question with one answer, a boolean, as a relation's value is everywhere else in the language, and a matrix and a number never compare silently; against, no element-wise comparison, which MATLAB's A == B gives as a matrix of booleans. The alternatives: a matrix of booleans would make a relation's value no boolean, and logic and piecewise would need matrices of booleans; an order of matrices has no meaning in linear algebra. Recommended: as taken, element-wise comparison as an operator or a function of its own, beside S246's element-wise operators, when a step asks for it. Taken.

S254. The face test grows in place: d_tests_matrices checks what the kind refuses and expr_transpose; seven new parts check the kind's arithmetic and each result's storage, the transpose, powers, indexing and relations as values, indexing and the transpose in the parser, in the renderer and in both folds, and simplification of matrices; and the last draws 1,000 random terms of matrices by a generator of its own, each folded and compared with the test's own arithmetic, by the definitions, element by element in the value's arithmetic apart from the kind and its kernels, powers by n - 1 products rather than by squaring, the same matrix exactly or a domain error where the generator drew shapes that do not fit. Its generator is its own, so the random parts before it draw what they drew; an oracle apart from the code tested catches what a round trip cannot, a wrong product that reads back as itself.

  • Alternative: the arithmetic checked against hand-computed matrices alone; or random matrices compared with the kind's own results under a second path, which would share the kernels' faults.

Review. Taken: for, an oracle apart from the kind, the definitions in the value's own arithmetic, so a wrong product that reads back as itself is caught; its powers by n - 1 products check the squaring; and every random part before it draws what it drew; against, the oracle shares value.h's arithmetic with the kind, so a fault in value.h would pass both. The alternatives: hand results alone would check a few shapes; a second path through the kernels would share their faults. Recommended: as taken; value.h's arithmetic is checked by its own tests. Taken.

S255. The checks: as S245, against chunk 34's delivered tree, with 648 planted faults: chunk 34's 569, 49 of them moved with the passages step 20 wrote again (six put in anew, their passages gone), and 79 in step 20's code, run in parts. The fuzz as chunk 34's, with a third part, 100,000 random terms of matrices checked against the fuzz's own arithmetic, dense ones among them; the depth probe with a fourth sweep, of nested indices and of transposes, and the bisection of chunk 34's finding with nested indices beside it; and the comparison with chunk 34's random texts, every line the same but those chunk 34 answered with step 20's not_yet, each of which must read otherwise now, and those whose value chunk 34 gave as a domain error of matrix arithmetic, whose term and fold in double must be the same.

  • Alternative: the full tier again, of which only the face test's 64 builds can tell this change from chunk 34's.

Review. Taken: as S245, the fuzz's matrices beside its terms and texts. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S256 to S278: chunk 36, 2026.10.26

The record: _handoffs/math98_chunk36_2026.10.26/README.md.

S256 to S264, chunk 36's, and S265 to S278, the owner's rulings of 2026.10.27 (2.7), as decisions.html has them; each is reviewed there (sections 2.6 and 2.7), the 22 outstanding decisions of S1 to S278 first, by group and urgency.

S256. Linear algebra's functions are functions of the language's table: det(A), inv(A), trace(A), norm(A) and eig(A) of one argument, dot(a, b), cross(a, b) and solve(A, B) of two, each called by name as sqrt is, each also a C++ function, expr_det to expr_solve. Of a matrix, det, inv, trace and eig take a square one; norm any, a vector's Euclidean norm and a matrix's Frobenius norm, one formula, the root of the sum of the squares of the elements; dot two vectors of one length, each a row or a column; cross two vectors of 3 elements, the result of the first's shape; solve(A, B) A square and B of its rows, or a vector of as many elements, X then of B's shape. A norm whose sum of squares, in floating point, passes its type's range or falls below its normal numbers' precision is computed again of the elements scaled exactly by a power of two, the norm scaled back, so that norm([1e200, 1e200]) is 1.414e200 and not an infinity, as LAPACK's dnrm2 and Julia's norm scale; elsewhere it is the kernels' norm, bit for bit. A number is a scalar, as in Julia: det, trace and eig of x are x, inv(x) is 1 / x, norm(x) is |x|, dot(x, y) is x y, solve(a, B) is B / a, and cross takes no number. In a type, where no value is a matrix, each is the scalar's, and cross a NaN, as a variable bound to a matrix is. As a value, a shape the function does not take, a boolean and none are domain errors, and an argument's error passes, the first; the C++ functions give bad_shape for a shape they do not take, with its message, as expr_matrix does. Julia's rule makes a 1 by 1 answer and a number agree, so a term written for matrices folds in a type, as every term must (S190's one grammar, two folds), and a scalar's det is the det of its 1 by 1 matrix.

  • Alternative: the functions of matrices alone, a number a domain error, as SymPy's Matrix.det takes no number; or a 1 by 1 result taken for its number (S246 refuses that); and the norm unscaled, as NumPy's linalg.norm and Eigen's norm() compute it, an infinity past the root of the type's range.

Review. Taken: for, every function has one meaning in each fold, a 1 by 1 answer and a number agree, and a term written for matrices folds in a type, as Julia's det(2), inv(2) and tr(2) give the scalar's answers; a norm the type holds is never an infinity or 0 for a square that overflowed or underflowed, and the scaling, by a power of two, changes no bit of a norm in range; against, cross of numbers is a NaN in a type and a domain error as a value, and a variable bound to a matrix folds in a type to a NaN, so a type cannot tell a matrix's det from a number's. The alternatives: functions of matrices alone would make det(x) of a number x an error in the one fold where every value is a number; a 1 by 1 result taken for its number is S246's refusal; the norm unscaled is NumPy's and Eigen's norm(), wrong at the type's ends. Recommended: as taken, with the same scaling in the C core's d_vec_norm and its _c form, where it is cheapest and serves every face, the language's wrapper then gone. Taken in chunk 38: the C core's norms, distance, unit vector and cosine scaled, the wrapper gone (S301).

S257. The square algebra, det, inv, solve and eig, is computed in one type, chosen from the matrix's storage: a dense matrix in its own type, by the C core: LU with partial pivoting for det and solve (decomp.h's d_decomp_lu, _lu_det, _lu_solve), Gauss-Jordan elimination with partial pivoting for inv (mat.h's d_mat_inverse, through the kernels); a matrix of exact values exactly, by the generic kernels in the value's own arithmetic: Bareiss's fraction-free elimination for det, Gauss-Jordan for inv, and solve as inv(A) B; a matrix of values with an inexact element in the widest inexact element's type, each exact element its nearest there, as the tower takes an operation's arguments; and a matrix with a boxed inexact element on the generic path, by its kind's own arithmetic. solve of two matrices takes the wider of their types, an exact one taking the other's. A singular matrix's inverse and solution are a pole, as an exact number over 0 is; its determinant is 0. Each result carries its operands' flags. trace, norm, dot and cross follow S247's rule, a matrix of values by the generic path, as the arithmetic does. An exact matrix's determinant and inverse are exact, which no floating-point library gives and every symbolic one does; a dense one's are LAPACK's algorithms, the C core's bits. The C core's LU reports a singular matrix only where a pivot is exactly 0, as LAPACK's dgesv reports it: a nearly singular dense matrix has an inverse of large elements, not a pole.

  • Alternative: every matrix in long double; or exact matrices in long double too, as NumPy computes every determinant.

Review. Taken: for, an exact matrix's determinant, inverse and solution exact, as SymPy's are and no floating-point library's are; a dense matrix's by LAPACK's algorithms, the C core's bits, in its own type; and a matrix mixed of exact and inexact elements computed as the tower computes an operation of them; against, a dense singular matrix is a pole only where a pivot is exactly 0, as LAPACK's dgesv reports it, so a nearly singular one has an inverse of huge elements and no warning. The alternatives: long double for every matrix would round an exact one and a float one otherwise than their types; NumPy's floating-point determinant of [1, 2; 3, 4] is -2.0000000000000004. Recommended: as taken, with an elimination of [A | B] in the kernels' families beside inverse (the C core's as d_mat_solve) for exact and boxed solves, and a condition estimate, LAPACK's rcond, as a function of its own, cond, when a step asks for one. Taken in chunk 38: the elimination of [A | B] in every family, the C++ face's solve on it (S302); cond when a step asks.

S258. eig(A) gives a square matrix's eigenvalues, ascending, as a vector of one row, in A's algebra type (S257): a symmetric A's by the cyclic Jacobi method (d_decomp_sym_eigen), A first scaled exactly by a power of two so that its largest magnitude is in [1, 2), the threshold its Frobenius norm times the type's epsilon; any other A's by Hessenberg QR with Wilkinson's shifts (d_decomp_eigenvalues, 1,000 iterations); NaNs of A's type where A holds a NaN or an infinity. Where they are not all found real the value is a domain error, as a complex pair has no value while the value has no complex kind (S45). An exact A's eigenvalues are found so in long double and each made exact where it is rational: A's characteristic polynomial is computed exactly, by Faddeev and LeVerrier; every rational eigenvalue is a multiple of 1/d, d the least common multiple of A's denominators (an eigenvalue being a root of a monic polynomial over the integers once A is scaled by d); so each found eigenvalue's nearest such multiple is tried as a root, exactly, and each root found is given to the nearest found eigenvalue not yet made exact, the polynomial divided by it once for each time it is a root. An exact A whose eigenvalues are each so found is answered even where long double found a complex pair, as it does for a defective A's repeated eigenvalue; irrational eigenvalues stay long doubles. So eig([2, 1; 1, 2]) is [1, 3], exact, as SymPy's eigenvals gives them, and [1, 1; -1, 3]'s eigenvalue 2, a double one that long double finds as a complex pair, is found twice, [2, 2]; a symmetric matrix's spectrum is real, and Jacobi's is accurate to its norm's epsilon.

  • Alternative: eigenvalues in long double alone, NumPy's; or exact algebraic numbers for every eigenvalue, SymPy's roots, which needs a kind of algebraic numbers.

Review. Taken: for, a symmetric matrix's eigenvalues by Jacobi, accurate to its norm's epsilon; any other's by the C core's Hessenberg QR; an exact matrix's rational eigenvalues exact, found as roots of its characteristic polynomial in exact arithmetic, each as often as it is repeated, so that a defective matrix's repeated eigenvalue, which floating-point QR finds as a complex pair, is answered; against, a real matrix with complex eigenvalues is a domain error, a dense defective or nearly repeated eigenvalue can be found as a complex pair and so be a domain error too, irrational eigenvalues of an exact matrix are long doubles, not exact algebraic numbers, and a boxed inexact matrix's are computed in long double, past which its kind may hold more digits. The alternatives: long double alone would round eigenvalues that are exact rationals; SymPy's exact roots need a kind of algebraic numbers. Recommended: as taken, with complex eigenvalues of the complex kind, as the owner ruled on 2026.10.27 (S265, S273), the C core's Francis's double-shift QR, done in chunk 38 (S292), finding each pair; the review's interim, a pair as two rows, is withdrawn. Taken in chunk 43, step 23's third part: every eigenvalue, complex where it is, a Hermitian matrix's real, an exact matrix's exact where it is a Gaussian rational (S352).

S259. A ^ (-n) of a square matrix is its inverse's power, inv(A) ^ n, exact for an exact A, in its type for a dense one, a pole where A is singular; to a floating-point -n as to a floating-point n (S248). pow(A, -n) is the same. S248's review asked for it once 3c brought the inverse.

  • Alternative: negative powers a domain error still, inv(A) ^ n written out.

Review. Taken: for, A ^ (-n) means what linear algebra means, inv(A) ^ n, as MATLAB and Julia compute it, a singular matrix's a pole; against, none. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S260. The empty matrix is 0 by 0, a matrix of values with no element, written [], which is also its literal, the term vector of no element. A shape of no row or no column but not neither stays bad_shape. Its arithmetic follows the rules of shapes: [] + [], -[], 2 * [], [] * [], []', [] ^ n, inv([]) and eig([]) are [], solve([], []) is [], det([]) is 1, trace([]), norm([]) and dot([], []) are 0; it equals itself alone; an index of it and cross of it are domain errors. These are the values Julia and NumPy give (det of the empty matrix is the empty product), and the empty matrix is what an operation that removes every row gives, a block skipped in a literal (S261).

  • Alternative: no empty matrix, as S241 had it; or empty matrices of every shape, n by 0 and 0 by n, as MATLAB and NumPy keep them, which a language whose literals have no shape of their own cannot write.

Review. Taken: for, the values Julia and NumPy give the empty matrix, det the empty product 1 and trace the empty sum 0, and a block that vanishes in a literal; against, one empty matrix, 0 by 0, where MATLAB and NumPy keep n by 0 and 0 by n, so the shape of an empty result, A's rows with none of its columns, is lost. The alternative, no empty matrix, would make every operation that removes all rows an error. Recommended: as taken; n by 0 shapes once an operation makes them (slices, null spaces), with a literal such as zeros(n, 0). Taken.

S261. A literal's elements are blocks: in a row, numbers and matrices stand beside one another, each of the first's rows, its columns theirs in turn; a matrix literal's rows stand one above another, each of the first's columns; so [A, b] is A with the column b beside it, [A; B] is A above B, and [[1, 2], 3; 4, 5, 6] is [1, 2, 3; 4, 5, 6]. A number is a block of 1 by 1, the empty matrix is skipped, and blocks that do not fit are a domain error, as in arithmetic of shapes. The parser can know a row's width only where each element is surely a number, a term of number atoms and no literal: it reports a ragged row, bad_shape at its end, only where the first row and the row are both such; otherwise the shapes are the fold's. In a type a literal of blocks has no value, as any literal. A literal of blocks is never square by its structure in simplification. MATLAB, Julia and Eigen's comma initializer write blocks so, and S241's review asked for them by 3c; a literal of numbers alone reads, prints and errs as it did.

  • Alternative: no blocks, a literal's elements numbers alone; or blocks in a function of their own, hcat and vcat as Julia names them.

Review. Taken: for, MATLAB's, Julia's and Eigen's block literals, [A, b] and [A; B], with every literal of numbers alone read, printed and refused as before; against, a ragged literal of variables, [x, 2; 3], is refused only when folded, a domain error, not bad_shape at the parse, since x may be bound to a matrix. The alternatives: no blocks would leave [A, b] to a function of its own; Julia's hcat and vcat are spellings beside the literal, not instead of it. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S262. The C core's d_mat_power squares as the language does: from the exponent's highest bit down, the power begun as A and at each bit below it squared, then multiplied by A on the left where the bit is 1; and so do the generic kernel's power and dynamic_matrix::power. One algorithm, so that a dense power is the same bits in the language, in linear_algebra and in the C core. S248's review asked for it; d_mat_power took n - 1 products, A ^ (k - 1) A, and now takes at most 2 log2(n). Its value changes in its last bits for n > 2, the change S248 recommended; t_mat.c checks it against the binary method and, exactly, against A A ^ (k - 1) where the products are exact.

  • Alternative: the C core's n - 1 products kept, the language's power differing from it (S248's flag).

Review. Taken: for, one algorithm for one power, so a dense power is the same bits in the language, in linear_algebra and in the C core, at 2 log2(n) products; against, d_mat_power's value for n > 2 changes in its last bits from chunk 35's, which a caller comparing bits with an earlier result would see. The alternative keeps S248's two answers. Recommended: as taken, S248's recommendation. Taken.

S263. The face test grows in place: its function table holds the eight functions; seven new parts check the functions of exact and dense matrices against the C core's algorithms bit for bit, and each storage's type; eigenvalues, exact, irrational, repeated, defective and complex; negative powers, and every dense power against d_mat_power, bit for bit; the empty matrix; literals of blocks in the parser, as values and in simplification; the functions in both folds; and 1,000 random exact matrices of 1 to 4 rows against the test's own arithmetic: det by cofactor expansion, inv and solve by A inv(A) = I and A x = b, each exact eigenvalue a root of det(A - x I), and a triangular matrix's eigenvalues its diagonal. t_mat.c checks d_mat_power against the binary method, and t_matrix_face.cpp the generic kernel's power against the C core's. The oracle is the definitions in the value's arithmetic, apart from the kernels, as S254's; its generator is its own.

  • Alternative: hand-computed matrices alone; or the random matrices compared with NumPy's answers, which the workspace cannot run in the queue.

Review. Taken: as S254, an oracle apart from the kernels, the definitions in the value's own arithmetic, with each dense type's algorithms checked bit for bit against the C core's own functions. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S264. The checks: as S255, against chunk 35's delivered tree, with 753 planted faults in math/expr/: chunk 35's 648, 22 of them moved with the passages step 21 wrote again, and 105 in step 21's code, run in parts; 11 more in the power by squaring of the C core, the kernels and dynamic_matrix, caught by their own tests; the full tier for the 28 tests of the module the C core's change reaches; the fuzz as chunk 35's, with a fourth part, random terms of the functions and of blocks against the fuzz's own arithmetic; and the comparison with chunk 35's random texts, every line the same but those chunk 35 answered as unknown functions or a ragged literal, each of which must read otherwise now.

  • Alternative: the face test's ladder alone, which cannot see the C core's change.

Review. Taken: as S255, with the full tier for the module's tests the changed headers reach, as the C core changed, and the power's faults in the C core, the kernels and dynamic_matrix beside math/expr/'s. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S265. Complex numbers and quaternions become kinds of the value, in three steps added to Phase 3 before Phase 4: 3d (step 22), the C core's complex numbers and the linear algebra they complete; 3e (step 23), the complex kind in the language; 3f (step 24), quaternions, in the C core and the language. The roadmap has 51 steps, Phase 4's 4a is step 25, and every step after it moves three further on. S265 to S278 are the owner's rulings of 2026.10.27, made during chunk 36 on its review's questions, and the design of those three steps; where he left a choice to the lane ("whatever allows the most accuracy, functionality and versatility ... the smallest chance of needing to be revised"), the ruling is the review's recommendation. Phase 4's derivative rules and simplification's declared domains (S274) are then written once, for the numbers they will keep. The stubs of math/expr/ name the old step numbers in their not_yet messages, as the test checks them, until step 22 renumbers both.

  • Alternative: complex numbers before Phase 4 and quaternions after it, 50 steps; or both after Phase 4, 4a's rules and simplification's guarantee then written again; or both left in the roadmap's "Later", eig's flag kept.
  • Alternative: no complex kind yet, each complex eigenvalue given as its real and imaginary parts, a matrix of two rows (S258's review's interim), eig's answer changing its shape now and again when the kind comes.

Review. Ruled: for, Phase 4's calculus written once for numbers that may be complex or quaternions, the eigenvalue flags (S45, S47, S49, S258) cleared within two steps, and no interim answer to revise; against, calculus three steps later, and the roadmap's step numbers changed, which the notes and the stubs' messages must follow. Recommended: as ruled. Ruled; steps 22 to 24.

S266. Every number kind past the real ones is made by one set of rules, complex numbers and quaternions first, intervals and the others of S278 after them, so that no kind revises another. Its parts are of one real kind: exact where they are given exact, and else of one floating-point type, the wider of its parts', as the tower takes an operation's operands. It is stored densely, as the C core's own type, where that type is float, double or long double, and as values otherwise, as a matrix is (S239). It has a rank of its own past the kinds it holds, a value of a kind below it taken into it exactly. An exact result is made the simplest kind that holds it exactly; an inexact one never is, its zero parts kept with their signs. Each function gives its principal value, with the branch cuts and signed zeros of C99's Annex G, after Kahan. Each operation is as accurate as the best published algorithm for it, its bound written in its header and checked against references of high precision; a _fast form is added only where a use asks for speed, rendering among them. The C core's type is a struct of C99, never _Complex, each function with its _c form; a C++ face over it, from C++98, converts to and from the standard library's type. A name the language reserves for a kind answers not_yet from the step before its own (S197). The rules are the lane's own, G2's exactness and G6's one answer, carried to each kind.

  • Alternative: each kind designed on its own, as libraries grew theirs: NumPy's complex numbers of doubles and floats alone, Eigen's quaternions of one floating-point type, SymPy's of symbols.

Review. Ruled: for, one design for every kind to come, so that none revises another; the lane's exactness and one answer (G2, G6) in each; a kind's dense storage the C core's own type, its bits the C core's; against, more work for each kind than one type of doubles, and a boxed allocation for an exact complex number or quaternion. Recommended: as ruled. Ruled; from step 22.

S267. The C core's complex numbers are a struct per floating-point type, d_complex_d and its float and long double kin, whose one member is an array of the two parts, real first, so that it has the layout of T[2], which C99's _Complex T and C++'s std::complex<T> share, and converts to either by a copy; passed and returned by value, as C99's functions pass theirs; in a header of its own in c/math/, made for the three families by tools/gen_c_math_float.py. Its functions are the 22 of C99's <complex.h>, creal to catanh, each in a _c form, a constant expression from C++14, and a plain one, both built on the C core's real functions and never on the C library's complex ones, with the real log1p, expm1, asinh, acosh and atanh they need, which the C core lacks (S203's review), each in both forms; conversions to and from _Complex are given where the compiler has it, and nothing needs them. C11 makes _Complex optional, MSVC has none, and C++ has no such type, yet the C core's headers compile as C++ too (the C++ ladder's 24 C headers); a struct of an array has no padding between its parts, which a struct of two members is not promised.

  • Alternative: _Complex, C99's own type: optional in C11, absent in MSVC and in C++.
  • Alternative: a struct of two members, re and im: the layout of T[2] on every ABI the lane meets, but not by the standard.
  • Alternative: the plain forms on the C library's cexp and the rest: absent where _Complex is, and of uneven quality.

Review. Ruled: for, one type in C99, C11 with or without _Complex, MSVC and C++, of the layout of both standard types; the plain and _c forms on the core's own real functions, so one algorithm everywhere; against, a struct, not the language's operators, in C. Chunk 37 names them struct d_cplx_d and its kin in cplx.h (S280). Recommended: as ruled. Ruled; step 22.

S268. The complex arithmetic and functions are as accurate as the best published algorithms, each bound in its header. A product's parts are each an exact product less another, rounded once, Kahan's way, within 2 ulps of each part (Jeannerod, Louvet and Muller), by fma where the target has it and Dekker's split where it has not and in a constant expression; a quotient is Baudin and Smith's robust division; the modulus a hypot scaled by a power of two, as S256 scales the norm. log near the unit circle takes log1p of the parts' squares formed exactly; asin, acos and their hyperbolic kin are Hull, Fairgrieve and Tang's; exp, sin, cos and the rest are formed of their real parts, scaled where a part would overflow; a whole power is repeated squaring, any other exp(w log z). The branch cuts and the principal values are C99's Annex G's, after Kahan: sqrt and log cut along the negative real axis, continuous from above, the sign of a zero imaginary part choosing the side, so that sqrt(-4 - 0i) is -2i; infinities and NaNs as Annex G gives them, a value with an infinite part an infinity whatever its other part. Each is checked against references of 50 digits or more, made by mpmath in the step's tools. This is the owner's "both" of his sixth answer, built with the kind.

  • Alternative: the textbook formulas, as many std::complexs compute: a part lost where products cancel, an overflow past about 1e154 in double where the result is in range, and infinities taken for NaNs.

Review. Ruled: for, each part's error bounded and stated, the cuts and special values of the standards every user of C, C++, Julia and Common Lisp meets; against, the cost: an exact product is two to four operations more than a plain one, Dekker's split more still, and the x87's long double fma is the library's software. Chunk 37 forms the product without fma in every build and the quotient by exact pairs (S281), and the plain pow's general case as the _c one (S283). Recommended: as ruled. Ruled; step 22.

S269. Complex numbers complete the C core's linear algebra, in step 22. A real general matrix's eigenvalues are found by Francis's double-shift QR, LAPACK's dlahqr, after balancing, LAPACK's dgebal, a diagonal similarity by powers of two, and given as complex numbers, each pair exactly conjugate and each real eigenvalue's imaginary part exactly +0; its eigenvectors come from the real Schur form by back-substitution, LAPACK's dtrevc, complex for a complex pair (S45's, S47's and S49's recommendations). A complex matrix has its LU with partial pivoting, determinant, inverse and solution; a Hermitian one its eigenvalues, real, by Jacobi's method with complex rotations; any other its eigenvalues by single-shift complex QR, LAPACK's zlahqr. The C core's norms scale as S256 recommends, d_vec_norm and its _c form, and with them normalize and the complex modulus; both kernel families, the C core's and the generic, have an elimination of [A | B] beside inverse (S257's). The generic kernels keep each product in its order, so that they hold for quaternions; and number_traits tells a field's traits apart from an order's, a magnitude to pivot by where a number has no <, taking S157's and S158's recommendations, an exact integer root and a relative tolerance, as the traits are written again. linear_algebra's eigen_general gives complex eigenvalues and eigenvectors beside the real ones it gives now.

  • Alternative: complex QR of a real matrix: four times the work, pairs not exactly conjugate, and real eigenvalues with imaginary parts of rounding, which an inexact complex number keeps (S266).
  • Alternative: LAPACK's two arrays of the parts, wr and wi: the same numbers, in two arrays where one type now holds them.

Review. Ruled: for, LAPACK's algorithms for each kind of matrix, pairs exactly conjugate and real eigenvalues exactly real, so that an inexact complex number's zero imaginary part is one, and four flags cleared (S45, S47, S49, S256) with S257's and S157's and S158's recommendations; against, the C core's largest step yet, four new kernels beside the complex functions. Recommended: as ruled. Ruled; step 22.

S270. math::complex<T>, of float, double and long double, from C++98, is the C core's struct's face, as math::value is the value's: its operators and functions are the C core's, the same bits on every compiler and at every level (G6), constexpr where the _c forms are, from C++14. It is made from a std::complex<T> implicitly and gives one by a member, not implicitly, so that no expression of the two types is ambiguous; and it has the number_traits of a field, so that linear_algebra's vectors and matrices of it compute. The standard's type is no face of the core, as its arithmetic is each standard library's own.

  • Alternative: std::complex<T> itself: each library's arithmetic, other bits on each, and no constexpr before C++20.

Review. Ruled: for, the core's bits through the face on every compiler, which std::complex cannot promise, and its constexpr from C++14; against, a second complex type beside the standard's, converted at the edges. Recommended: as ruled. Ruled; step 22.

S271. The language's complex kind, in step 23, is a boxed kind of rank 900, past every real kind's and below the matrix's 1000, in the room S238 left, its parts, storage and exactness as S266 makes them. im, a constant of the table, is its imaginary unit, 3 + 4im by implied multiplication; complex(x, y) is the number of those parts, as C11's CMPLX makes the values a sum cannot, a signed zero or an infinity in one part. real, imag, conj and arg are functions of the table, and abs and the bars give the modulus. Every function of the table that has a principal complex value takes a complex argument: sqrt, exp, exp2, log, log2, log10, pow and ^, the trigonometric and hyperbolic functions and their inverses, log1p, expm1, asinh, acosh and atanh joining the table; those of the real line's order, cbrt, floor, ceil, round, trunc, fmod, remainder, mod, atan2, hypot and the factorial, give a domain error of a non-real one. = and != compare the parts; <, <=, > and >= are a domain error of any complex operand, as in Julia and Python. Each is exact wherever its value is a Gaussian rational: (1 + 2im) (3 - im) is 5 + 5im and sqrt(2im) is 1 + im. In a floating-point type im is a NaN, as sqrt(-1) is there; and the folds take math::complex<T> as a type of their own, each function its complex form there. A complex number is written 3 + 4im, bracketed where its place asks, and reads back as a term of its value. im, jm and km (S275) answer not_yet from step 22. i would be lost as a variable's name, and A[i, j] with it.

  • Alternative: i, a constant of the table, never again a variable (S202); or a suffix, 2i, which reads today as 2 times i (S197).
  • Alternative: complex numbers ordered by their real parts, or lexicographically, as NumPy sorts them: an order no field has.
  • Alternative: no fold in math::complex<T>, the value's alone, each number boxed.

Review. Ruled: for, a complex number of every kind the tower has, exact Gaussian rationals among them; im costing one variable's name, where i would cost every index; the folds in math::complex<T> as fast as the real ones; the relations refusing what no field orders; against, an exact complex number is boxed and allocates, and a variable named im is lost. Recommended: as ruled. Ruled; step 23.

S272. A real function of a real argument outside its real domain gives its principal complex value as a value: sqrt(-4) is 2im, exactly, log(-1) is pi im, and (-8) ^ (1/3) is 1 + 1.732...im, as MATLAB, Mathematica, SymPy and Python's ** give them; cbrt(-8) stays -2, the real root, and in a floating-point type each is C's NaN, as now. An option of the evaluation's context, expr_context, makes them domain errors instead, as Julia's and C's are, for a computation meant to stay real; it comes with the kind, in step 23. The value's kind already follows its value, 1/3 a rational and a product of matrices a matrix; so a complex result where no real one exists is the tower's rule carried one kind further.

  • Alternative: a domain error unless the argument is already complex, as Julia and C give it, sqrt(complex(-4)) for 2im.

Review. Ruled: for, every function answered where a value exists, as the systems the language is measured against answer; the tower's rule carried to complex values; and an option for a computation meant to stay real; against, where the option is off a real computation that strays negative goes complex silently, and ^ gives the principal root, 1 + 1.732...im for (-8) ^ (1/3), where cbrt gives -2. Recommended: as ruled, the option given with the kind's functions, in step 23's second part, chunk 41 (S324), which gave it, expr_context's real_results (S340). Ruled 2026.11.04: S272 follows S340's ruling, the option on each function's evaluator, made in chunk 46 (S385). Flag: significant drawback, documented: a stray negative goes complex silently unless the option is set.

S273. Matrices of complex numbers, in step 23, are dense where every element is a floating-point number of one type, complex or real, the C core's structs in a row-major array, as LAPACK lays them, and values otherwise: S239's storages and three more. A' is the conjugate transpose, as in MATLAB and Julia, the same as now for every real matrix, and transpose(A) the transpose; dot(a, b) is the sum of conj(a_i) b_i, as Julia's dot, NumPy's vdot and BLAS's zdotc, so that dot(a, a) is norm(a) ^ 2; norm is of the moduli, scaled (S256), and cross conjugates nothing. det, inv, solve and trace are S269's kernels' or exact; eig of a Hermitian matrix is Jacobi's, real, of a real one Francis's QR's, and of any other complex QR's. Eigenvalues ascend by real part, then by imaginary part, as Julia sorts them, a conjugate pair side by side, the negative first; a real spectrum is real, as now (S258), and a spectrum with a complex pair complex throughout, each real eigenvalue's imaginary part +0. An exact matrix's eigenvalues that are Gaussian rationals are exact, eig([0, -1; 1, 0]) being [-im, im]: d A's characteristic polynomial is monic over the Gaussian integers, so each such eigenvalue's parts are multiples of 1/d, and the nearest such candidate of each eigenvalue found is tried exactly, as S258 tries the rational ones; the rest are long doubles.

  • Alternative: A' the transpose, as now, and the conjugate transpose a function of its own, as NumPy's .T and .conj().T are: unlike MATLAB's and Julia's A', which their users write for the adjoint.
  • Alternative: dot unconjugated, NumPy's dot: dot(a, a) then not a norm's square.
  • Alternative: eigenvalues unsorted, as LAPACK, NumPy and MATLAB give them: their order the algorithm's.

Review. Ruled: for, the conjugate transpose and the conjugating dot of MATLAB, Julia and BLAS, the same as today's for every real matrix; each kind of matrix its own algorithm; eigenvalues in an order of their own, a real spectrum real; exact eigenvalues wherever they are Gaussian rationals; against, A' and dot of complex matrices differ from NumPy's .T and dot. Recommended: as ruled. Ruled; step 23, made in chunk 43 (S348 to S352).

S274. Simplification's variables have declared domains, in step 25 (4a), widening S251's recommendation. A variable not declared is a complex number, which commutes, so that no rewrite changes a value at any complex binding, as each of today's rules already holds over the complex numbers; simplification's guarantee is then of the complex numbers, not the real. A declaration makes a variable real, positive or an integer, a rule true only there then applying to it, sqrt(x ^ 2) being |x| of a real x and x of a positive one; or a complex number; or a quaternion, or a matrix of a shape where known, these two keeping their order among the factors as their literals do (S251).
