S324 to S334: chunk 40, 2026.10.31

The record: _handoffs/math98_chunk40_2026.10.31/README.md.

S324 to S334, chunk 40's, as decisions.html has them; each is reviewed there, none flagged; S134's review notes its close (S326), and S203's, S258's, S272's and S322's name the part of step 23 that brings them; and the 18 outstanding decisions of S1 to S334 come first, by group and urgency, as chunk 39's made them (section 2.6). The text below is decisions_c40.md, which decisions_c40.py reads.

S324. Step 23 is made in three chunks, each delivered and checked whole, as S279 made step 22: chunk 40 the complex kind of the value and what a number of it is in the language, its parts, arithmetic, modulus, comparison, conversion and text, im's value, complex, real, imag, conj and arg, z', the relations, the exact powers to a whole exponent, and the holders every kind counts (S134); chunk 41 each function's principal complex value (S271), the five functions chunk 37 gave the C core and min, max and sign joining the table (S203), a real function's complex value outside its real domain with expr_context's option (S272), and the folds in math::complex<T>; chunk 42 matrices of complex numbers (S273), their storage, A', transpose, dot, the norms and eig complex and ordered (S258), and the C++ face's conjugating dot, complex norms and is_unitary (S322). Each part is a chunk the record can check whole, its references and faults with it; what a later part computes answers not_yet meanwhile, by the step's message (S331).

  • Alternative: step 23 in one chunk: the kind, thirty functions' complex values and matrices of complex numbers checked together, the largest change of the language yet in one delivery.

Review. Taken: for, each part checked whole, its references and faults with it, and what a later part computes answering not_yet, never a value it would change; the holders atomic before the first kind that shares a box between folds; against, three deliveries for one step, and two chunks in which a complex number has no square root. Recommended: as taken. Taken. Chunk 41 made the second part in two chunks, the C core's five complex functions it lacks the second's (S335), so that the third part is chunk 43, which made it, so that step 23 is done (S353).

S325. Whether a boxed value is exact is asked of each value: value.h's struct d_math_boxed_kind gains a last entry, exact, which a kind that is a number, but not exact in all its values, sets, and which is asked only of a number of a kind whose traits do not say D_MATH_BOXED_EXACT; NULL means none of its values is exact. A table written before it, with one initializer fewer, holds NULL there, as C and C++ initialize a member no initializer names; the wide integer's, the matrix's and the harnesses' tables name it. A complex number of exact parts is exact and one of doubles is not, by S266's one set of rules, and so will a quaternion and an interval be: exactness is the value's, as its parts are, while one kind keeps one table, as the matrix keeps one for every storage (S238).

  • Alternative: two kinds, an exact complex number of rank 900 and an inexact one of 910, as the tower's inline kinds are exact or not by kind: no change to value.h, but two tables for each kind to come, and cross-kind cases within one kind of number.
  • Alternative: D_MATH_VALUE_FLAG_INEXACT on every inexact complex number: a flag every result inherits, so that an exact result computed from one would be marked inexact.

Review. Taken: for, exactness asked where it lives, of the value, so that one kind of complex numbers, quaternions or intervals keeps one table, as the matrix does for every storage (S238); a table written before the entry unchanged in meaning; against, a change to the C core's table, and an indirect call for each exactness asked of such a kind. The alternatives: two kinds for each number of S266's rules, and cases between them; or a flag that every result inherits. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S326. A box's holders are counted atomically by default, closing S134's flag: value.h's d_math_boxed_hold and d_math_boxed_unhold change the size_t every box already has by GCC's __atomic builtins, which Clang and the GCC-compatible compilers give at every language level, C89 and C++98 among them, or by MSVC's interlocked functions; the increment relaxed, the decrement with acquire and release, so that the last holder sees every other's reads before it gives the box back, as shared_ptr's count is kept. Where a compiler has neither, the count is plain and D_MATH_BOXED_HOLDERS_ATOMIC is 0. The knob D_CFG_MATH_VALUE_ATOMIC_HOLDERS, in a new config/c/math/cfg_value.h, on by default, turns it off for a program that never shares a value between threads; cfg_value.h resolves which way the count is kept, and value.h reads the answer, as dconfig's localization rule asks. The wide integer, the matrix and the complex number count through them; so will every kind to come. The language's constant im is one box, held by every fold that reads it: two threads evaluating 3 + 4im at once change its count together.

  • Alternative: C11's _Atomic size_t and C++11's std::atomic: types of their own, which no struct C and C++ both read can hold, and none below C11 or C++11.
  • Alternative: plain by default, atomic behind the knob: a data race, undefined behaviour, for every program that shares a value and does not know to set it.
  • Alternative: a lock around each count: a lock for every copy of a value.

Review. Taken: for, values shared between threads safe by default, at every language level the lane serves, with no type the C and C++ structs cannot both hold; the count's orders shared_ptr's; a knob for a single-threaded program; against, an atomic step on every copy, a few cycles uncontended, and a compiler with neither builtins nor MSVC's functions plain, which D_MATH_BOXED_HOLDERS_ATOMIC says. The alternatives: C11's and C++11's atomic types, which no struct both languages read can hold; plain by default, a data race for every program that shares a value unawares; a lock for every copy. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S327. The language's complex numbers are one boxed kind, "complex", of rank 900, written in C++ from C++11 in math/expr/complex.hpp, beside the matrix kind, as the language that makes them is (S237). Its parts are exact, integers, rationals or wide integers, kept as two math::values, where both are given exact; and otherwise dense, a math::complex<float>, <double> or <long double>, the C core's struct, in the wider of the parts' floating-point types, each part its nearest there (S266); a part of a boxed kind that is not exact, which no kind is yet, keeps both as values. An exact complex number whose imaginary part comes to 0 is its real part, of a real kind, as a rational of denominator 1 is an integer; an inexact one keeps its parts, its zeros with their signs, never made real. The kind is a number, its exactness its parts' (S325). In C++, expr_complex(re, im) makes one as the language's complex(x, y) does, expr_is_complex asks for one, expr_real, expr_imag, expr_conj and expr_arg are the language's functions of a number, and expr_complex_of and expr_complex_to go to and from a math::complex<T>. A part of complex(1/3, 0.5) is the double nearest 1/3, as the tower takes 1/3 + 0.5; complex(3, 0) is 3, and complex(3.0, 0.0) stays 3 + 0im.

  • Alternative: the kind in C, c/math/value_cplx.h, beside the wide integer: a complex value for C programs, which have no language to make one with, and its exact parts' boxes held by hand.
  • Alternative: dense parts always, an exact part its nearest long double: (1 + 2im)(3 - im) no longer exactly 5 + 5im, against S271.

Review. Taken: for, the kind beside the matrix, in the language that makes it, its exact parts held as C++ objects hold their boxes (S237); exact numbers exact and floating-point ones the C core's struct, as S266 rules; an exact number with no imaginary part a real one, the tower's simplest kind; against, an exact complex number allocates a box with two values, and C programs have no complex value. The alternatives: the kind in C, for callers who have no language to make one with, each part's box held by hand; dense parts always, exactness lost. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S328. Its arithmetic is the value's, through its table, with a complex operand or a real one of any lower rank. Exact operands compute on the parts by value.h's exact operations, (a + bi)(c + di) as (ac - bd) + (ad + bc)i and a quotient over c^2 + d^2, a part past the family G2's long double, flagged, and the number then of long double parts; any other operation computes in the widest floating-point type of its operands, each exact part its nearest there, by cplx.h's arithmetic through math::complex<T>, a real operand taken as itself by the _real forms (C99's G.5), so that (1 + 0im)(-1) is -1 - 0im. An exact quotient by an exact 0 is a pole, as a real one is. -z negates both parts; |z|, abs and the bars, is of exact parts the root of the exact sum of their squares, exact where it is a rational square, |3 + 4im| being 5, and otherwise the long double nearest the root of the long double nearest the sum, as norm([c, d]) is (S257); and of dense parts cplx.h's abs_c. arg is cplx.h's arg_c in the parts' type, exact parts' long doubles. The kind's arithmetic is cplx.h's one form, which has no plain one, and its modulus and argument the _c forms, as math::complex<T>'s are (S313), so that a value and the face give one answer.

  • Alternative: a real operand taken as x + 0i, as the textbook writes it: (1 + 0im)(-1) is -1 + 0im, its zero's sign lost, and an infinite operand meeting a zero is a NaN.
  • Alternative: the modulus of exact parts as hypot of their nearest long doubles: two roundings before the root, where the exact sum takes one.
  • Alternative: the plain forms of abs and arg, as the language's real functions are (S194): bits that vary with the C library, as S313 found.

Review. Taken: for, exact arithmetic exact, G2 where a part passes the family, as every exact kind has it; floating-point arithmetic cplx.h's, a real operand as itself, a zero's sign kept, as C99's G.5 and math::complex keep it; the modulus of exact parts rounded once from the exact sum, the norm's answer; the modulus and argument of dense parts the _c forms', one answer with the face (S313); against, the language's real functions are the plain forms (S194), so abs(z) and hypot(real(z), imag(z)) of dense parts may differ in a last bit. The alternatives: a real operand as x + 0i, a sign lost; the modulus of rounded parts, two roundings; the plain forms, the C library's bits. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S329. A complex number equals a number whose parts are each equal to its own, a real number's imaginary part being 0, in value.h's exact order, so that 1/2 + im equals 0.5 + 1.0im and 3 - 0.0im equals 3; it stands in no other order with any number, a NaN part with itself too. It converts to a float, a double or a long double where its imaginary part is 0, of either sign, as its real part's nearest, with the rounding's direction found exactly; never to an integer or a rational, as no exact complex number is real. The language's = and != of a complex number are by its parts; <, <=, > and >= a domain error (S271). A dense complex number of imaginary part 0 is a real number, and a fold in a real type reads it as one, as Julia's Float64(3.0 + 0.0im) is 3.0.

  • Alternative: no conversion at all, as Python's float(3 + 0j) is an error: a variable bound to 3 + 0.0im a NaN in a real type.
  • Alternative: the real part always, as NumPy converts with a warning: an imaginary part dropped silently.

Review. Taken: for, equality by the parts in the exact order, across kinds, as the real numbers compare; no order where no field has one; a complex number of imaginary part 0 a real one to a fold in a real type, as Julia converts; against, 3 + 0.0im converts to 3.0 though its kind is complex, which a reader of Python would not expect. The alternatives: no conversion, a variable bound to 3 + 0.0im a NaN in a real type; the real part always, an imaginary part dropped silently. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S330. The value layer writes a complex number 3 + 4im: its real part, then + or -, then its imaginary part's magnitude and im; exact parts as integers or fractions, 1/2 - 3/4im, floating-point parts in max_digits10 digits as a matrix's elements are, and a part that is no finite number by name, nan + inf*im. The language's renderer writes it as a sum, in brackets wherever it stands, as it writes a negative number or a fraction, (3 + 4 * im), (1.5 - 0 * im), each part as a number with no literal is written but bare, the least d_math_imax as the next one less 1; so that it reads back as a term of its value with implied multiplication off, as it is by default (S196), or on. S271 writes 3 + 4im, as a person types it with implied multiplication on; the renderer knows no syntax, and spells the product as it spells every one. An inexact number reads back as itself in its type, its signed zeros among them, where a fold in math::complex<T> computes the sum (chunk 41).

  • Alternative: (3 + 4im): no text the default syntax reads.
  • Alternative: complex(3, 4), which reads back a part's -0 or infinity exactly as a value too: a call where every system writes a sum.

Review. Taken: for, the value layer's text as every system writes a complex number; the renderer's a sum that reads back with implied multiplication off, as by default, or on, bracketed as a negative number is wherever it stands, its parts as numbers with no literal are written; against, the renderer's 4 * im where S271 writes 4im, and brackets at the top of a text where none are needed, as a negative number has them. The alternatives: (3 + 4im), which the default syntax does not read; complex(3, 4), a call where every system writes a sum. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S331. What step 23's later parts compute answers not_yet meanwhile, by the fold, after an operand's error and wrong_arity, its message naming the part: of the second, a function of a complex argument whose principal complex value it gives, sqrt, the trigonometric and hyperbolic functions and their inverses, exp, exp2, log, log2 and log10, and a power of a complex number but an exact one's to a whole exponent, which is exact; of the third, a literal with a complex element, an operation of a matrix and a complex number, linear algebra's functions of one, and complex, real, imag, conj and arg of a matrix. S332. In a floating-point type every number is real: im is a NaN, as sqrt(-1) is there (S271); complex(x, y) is x where y is 0, of either sign, and a NaN otherwise; real(x) is x, imag(x) +0, conj(x) x, and arg(x) carg(x + 0i), pi where x is negative or -0, a NaN for a NaN, and +0 otherwise, pi the constant table's in the type. As a value, arg of an exact real number not negative is exactly 0, 0 among them, and of a negative one pi in long double. arg(0) is 0, as C99's carg (G.6), Julia's angle, Python's cmath.phase, MATLAB's angle and Mathematica's Arg give it.

  • Alternative: complex(x, y) a NaN in a type whatever y is: complex(x, 0) no longer x, as it is as a value.
  • Alternative: arg(0) a domain error, as atan2(0, 0) is as a value: every system's 0 refused.

Review. Taken: for, every number in a type real, as S271 rules for im; complex(x, 0) x in a type as it is as a value; arg(x) carg(x + 0i), as C99 gives it; arg(0) 0, every system's; against, arg(0) is exactly 0 where atan2(0, 0) is a domain error as a value (S203's table). The alternatives: complex(x, y) a NaN whatever y is; arg(0) a domain error. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S333. A complex number's transpose, z', is its conjugate, as A' is the conjugate transpose (S273) and a number is a 1 by 1 matrix, as MATLAB's and Julia's ' are; a real number's is itself, as now. An exact complex number to a whole power is exact, by the repeated squaring the real powers take, (1 + im) ^ 2 being 2im and (1 + im) ^ (-2) -1/2im; pow is ^.

  • Alternative: z' the number itself, a number its own transpose: A' of a 1 by 1 complex matrix then other than A[1]'.

Review. Taken: for, z' the adjoint of a 1 by 1 matrix, as MATLAB's and Julia's ' are, so that A' of a complex matrix will agree with each element's; exact powers exact by the squaring the real ones take; against, z' differs from NumPy's transpose of a number. The alternative: z' the number, then other than A' of the matrix [z]. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S335 to S341: chunk 41, 2026.11.01

S335. S336. A function's complex value is math::complex<T>'s, so cplx.h's _c form, as a value and in a complex type alike, as the kind's modulus and argument already are (S328) and the face's every operation is (S313); a value's computes in the widest floating-point type of its arguments, a long double where every one is exact, dense and never made real (S266). The language's real functions keep their plain forms (S194). So that a complex value of the language and math::complex<T> give the same bits, and a fold in math::complex<T> the same as the value's of the same arguments.

  • Alternative: the plain forms for complex values, as the real functions take them: faster on a fast C library, and bits that differ from the face's and between libraries, cplx.h's plain bounds running to 6 steps.
  • Alternative: the plain forms in a complex type and the _c forms as values: two answers for one computation.

Review. Recommended: as taken, a _fast form where a use asks for one (S266). Taken. Ruled 2026.11.04 (the owner's): the second alternative, the plain forms in a complex type and the _c forms as values, two answers for one computation, which the owner chose over one answer, the docs saying where a value and math::complex<T> may differ. Made in chunk 46 (S384).

S337. A function of exact arguments is exact wherever its value is a Gaussian rational: sqrt(z) and z ^ (p/q) where the principal q-th root of z is one, sqrt(2im) being 1 + im, sqrt(-4) 2im and (-4) ^ (3/2) -8im, then raised to p exactly; and sign(z) where |z| is rational, sign(3 + 4im) being 3/5 + 4/5im. The root is found as a Gaussian integer: with z = N / D, N a Gaussian integer and D whole, D times a root of z is a root of M = z D^q, an algebraic integer, so a Gaussian integer where it is a Gaussian rational; M's principal root is computed in long double, its parts rounded to whole numbers, and taken where its q-th power is M exactly and it lies within 2^-10 of its modulus of the principal root, each other root lying 2 sin(pi / q) of it away. Where a term or a power passes the family, there is none. No other function of a complex Gaussian rational is one (Lindemann and Weierstrass, Gelfond and Schneider), so no other exact value is lost.

  • Alternative: sqrt alone exact, by |z|: re = sqrt((|z| + a) / 2) and im = sign(b) sqrt((|z| - a) / 2), each exactly: no exact root but the square root, (-2 + 2im) ^ (1/3) being 1 + im among those lost.
  • Alternative: exactness for whole powers alone, as chunk 40 left it: sqrt(-4) a long double 2im.

Review. Taken: for, every exact root and rational power the Gaussian rationals have, an estimate taken only where its power is verified exactly, so that no exact value is ever wrong; sign exact where the modulus is; against, a long double estimate and an exact power for each root, and roots whose terms pass the family dense. The alternatives: the square root alone exact; whole powers alone. Recommended: as taken. Taken. Ruled 2026.11.04: as taken.

S338. min and max take any number of arguments from 1, as Julia's, Python's and Mathematica's do; in a floating-point type they are IEEE 754-2019's minimum and maximum, a NaN where any argument is one and -0 below +0, as Julia's and NumPy's minimum are; as a value, the least or the greatest argument itself, by value.h's exact order, so that an exact argument stays exact, min(1/3, 0.5) being 1/3 as in Mathematica and SymPy, the first of equal ones but a -0 below a 0, a NaN where any is one, every argument's flags kept; of a complex number a domain error. sign(x) is 1 or -1 by its sign, a 0 or a NaN itself, as Julia's is, exact for an exact x; and sign(z) of a complex z is z / |z|, 0 at 0 (S203).

  • Alternative: C's fmin and fmax, of two arguments, a NaN ignored, as MATLAB's min ignores one: a NaN that vanishes from a result.
  • Alternative: the tower's kind for min and max, as Julia promotes min(1//3, 0.5) to 0.333...: an exact argument made inexact.

Review. Taken: for, IEEE 754-2019's operations, a NaN never lost and -0 ordered; an exact argument kept exact (G2); sign Julia's, a zero's sign kept; against, a user of C's fmin or MATLAB's min meets a NaN where those drop it, and min of an exact and an inexact argument may be the exact one, not the tower's kind. The alternatives: a NaN dropped from a result; an exact argument made inexact. Recommended: as taken, a NaN-dropping form where a use asks for one. Taken. Ruled 2026.11.04: as taken, and the docs say how min and max differ from fmin; made in chunk 45 (S379).

S339. The folds take math::complex<T> for T float, double and long double, as S271 rules: expr_evaluate<math::complex<T>>. A real number stays real there, as in the value: the fold carries with each number whether it is real, so that a term whose every number is real has its bits in T, plus +0i; an operator of real operands is T's, and of a complex operand the face's, a real one taken as itself (G.5); a function of real arguments is its real evaluator's, but where that is a NaN of arguments that are not, its principal complex value at x + 0i (S272), so that sqrt(-4) is 2i; of a complex argument its complex evaluator's. expr_function gains an evaluator for each complex type, which every standard function has, and a caller's function without one is its real evaluator's of real arguments and a NaN otherwise; expr_named_constant gains a value in each complex type, im's 0 + 1i. In a complex type x' is the conjugate (S333), = and != compare the parts, <, <=, >, >=, mod and ! of a complex number are a NaN, and linear algebra's functions of a number not real answer not_yet, the third part's.

  • Alternative: every number complex in the fold, as std::complex arithmetic is: -4 there -4 - 0i, so that sqrt(-4) is -2i where the value's is 2im, and a real term's bits other than T's, x ^ 2 by cpow.
  • Alternative: no fold in a complex type, the value's alone (S271's alternative), each number boxed.

Review. Taken: for, the fold S271 rules; a real term's bits the same in T and in math::complex<T>, and a complex value's the same as the value's, sqrt(-4) 2i in both; a caller's table working there unchanged, its functions of real arguments; against, a flag carried with each number, a real term's zeros +0 where the value has none, and an operation of a complex number and a real one keeping the zeros' signs C99's G.5 gives, not those of x + 0i. The alternatives: every number complex, as std::complex computes, two answers for sqrt(-4); or no fold in a complex type. Recommended: as taken. Taken. Ruled 2026.11.04: as taken.

S340. S272's option is expr_context's first field, real_results, false by default: where it is true, an operation of real operands whose value is complex alone, complex(x, y) apart, is a domain error, of exact and inexact operands alike, sqrt(-4) and sqrt(-4.0) both; and so are log2 and log10 of a negative number and log1p of one below -1. The fold applies it, after the function, as it alone has the context. The typed folds have no context: in a real type each such value is a NaN, as C's is, and in a complex type the principal complex value.

S341. A real argument on a branch cut is taken as x + 0i, its zero part +0, so that its value is the one C99's Annex G gives on the cut's upper side, as C, Python's cmath, NumPy and Scilab give it: asin(2) is pi/2 + 1.317...im, acos(2) -1.317...im, atanh(2) 0.549... + pi/2 im and acosh(1/2) 1.047...im; sqrt and log of a negative number take the upper side by every convention. MATLAB takes the cuts of asin and acos past 1 from below: its asin(2) is 1.5708 - 1.3170i, the imaginary part the opposite of Scilab's, as Scilab's own comparison of the two says.

  • Alternative: MATLAB's side for asin and acos past 1: a real argument's value then no longer Annex G's at x + 0i, and a value of the language other than math::complex<T>'s at the same point.

Review. The alternative: MATLAB's side, a value of the language other than the face's at the same point. Recommended: as taken, documented. Taken. S342 to S347: chunk 42, 2026.11.02

The record: _handoffs/math98_chunk42_2026.11.02/README.md.

S342 to S347, chunk 42's, as decisions.html has them; each is reviewed there, none flagged. The 18 outstanding decisions of S1 to S347 come first, by group and urgency (section 2.5); S258 and S322 are due in chunk 43. The text below is decisions_c42.md, which decisions_c42.py reads.

S342. cplx.h gains exp2, log2, log10, log1p and expm1 of a complex number, each one long double kernel of pure arithmetic, its parts carried as pairs to past twice the value's precision and rounded once; and both forms of every family are that kernel rounded to the family, the _c form as every other's is, and the plain form too, as standard C's library has none of the five to be faster with: C99 reserves the names cexp2, cexpm1, clog10, clog1p and clog2 for them, and glibc and the UCRT give clog10 alone, as an extension (S266). log2 and log10 are log |z| and arg z as pairs, times 1/ln b as a pair; log1p's log |1 + z| is half the logarithm of 1 + w, w = 2x + x^2 + y^2 summed exactly, where |1 + z|^2 lies in [1/2, 2], and of the squares of 1 + x as a pair and y elsewhere, its angle with 1 + x as a pair; expm1's real part is (e^x - 1)

  • 2 e^x sin^2(y/2) within ln 2 / 2 of the imaginary axis and e^x cos y - 1 past it (S345); exp2's angle y ln 2 is reduced exactly (S344). Each part within a step of the part correctly rounded, in every family and both forms, at every reference point.
  • Alternative: the textbook formulas in each family's type, log z / ln b, exp(z ln 2), log(1 + z), and expm1(x) cos y - 2 sin^2(y/2) beside e^x sin y: fast; a step or two off where a value already rounded is divided or multiplied by ln b; for log1p, many steps off near |1 + z| = 1 and every bit of a small z once 1 + z rounds to 1; every bit of expm1's real part where it cancels near the curve e^x cos y = 1; and every bit of exp2's angle past |y| of some 2^53, where y ln 2 is rounded.
  • Alternative: plain forms of their own, in each family's type on the C library's real functions, falling back to the kernel where they would lose: faster for a C caller, a second set of algorithms per family to bound and test, and the cancellations to detect.

Review. Taken: for, every family's parts within a step of the correctly rounded ones at every reference point, both forms one answer, independent of the C library; against, the plain forms no faster than the _c ones, and the pairs' kernels slower than C99's formulas would be. The alternatives: fast values wrong by many steps where they cancel, or every bit; or a second set of algorithms per family. Recommended: as taken, a _fast form where a use asks for one (S266). Taken.

S343. On the real axis, where the value is real - every x for exp2 and expm1, x positive for log2 and log10, x past -1 for log1p -- each of the five, in both forms and every family, is that family's real function in the same form, the _c form's _c and the plain form's plain, with y's zero; and on the axes elsewhere the kernel takes the real function of |x| or |y| for log2's and log10's real part, so that a power of the base gives its exponent exactly: log2(-8) is 3 + 4.532...i. So that log2(x + 0i) is log2(x) bit for bit, in C, the face and the language alike.

  • Alternative: the kernel on the axis too, rounded to the family: one path, its float and double values there rounded twice, a step from the real function's at rare points, and the plain form's never the C library's.

Review. Taken: for, a real argument's value one answer in C, the face and the language, the real function's; a power of the base exact; against, a test per family before the kernel, and the plain forms' values on the axis the C library's, as every plain real function's are. The alternative: values on the axis a step from the real function's at rare points. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S344. exp2's angle, y ln 2, is reduced by pi/2 exactly for every y: below 1 in magnitude as a pair, ln 2 held as the exponential holds it, the angle then within pi/4; past it by Payne and Hanek's method, as elementary.h reduces x, with 2 ln 2 / pi's digits in 2/pi's place - a table of 704 digits of 24 bits, 16,896 bits, which the generator computes from Machin's formula for pi and 2 atanh(1/3) for ln 2, both ends of their errors agreeing at the cut. So that exp2(x + iy) is within a step of its value at every y a family has, 2^118 and 10^300 among them.

  • Alternative: y ln 2 as a pair, ln 2 in two parts or three: exact to |y| of some 2^30 or 2^60, and past it every bit of the angle lost, as NumPy's and Julia's exp(z ln 2) lose it from 2^53.
  • Alternative: exp2(z) as pow(2, z), whose w log z carries ln 2 as its pair: the same limit.

Review. Taken: for, every y, the reduction as exact as the core's sine's and cosine's; against, a second table of 2,112 bytes in cplx.h and a pass of Payne and Hanek's for every |y| of 1 or more. The alternatives: a limit on y past which the angle is noise. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S345. expm1's real part, which vanishes on the curve e^x cos y = 1, is formed of terms good to some 2^-(P+28) of themselves: e^x - 1 by the exponential's series in pairs to its eighth term, e^x by the same on x less k ln 2, ln 2 in three parts, and the sine and cosine of the reduced angle by their series in pairs to their twelfth terms, written in cplx.h's section 3.1; elementary.h's pairs round each term in long double, some 2^-(P+3) of the value, and power.h's exponential of a pair is good to some 2^-(P+18). Within some 2^-28 of the curve, relatively, the real part keeps its precision; nearer, its error is some 2^-(P+28) of its terms, as any fixed precision's is somewhere.

  • Alternative: elementary.h's and power.h's pairs as they are: the real part far from its value within 2^-10 of the curve.
  • Alternative: the terms in triple pairs, to some 2^-(2P+20): the curve met within 2^-60, for three times the work, at points a random argument meets one time in 2^28.

Review. Taken: for, the real part within a step everywhere but within some 2^-28 of the curve, relatively, where any fixed precision ends somewhere; the series written once, in the five's section; against, two series in cplx.h beside elementary.h's and power.h's, and points nearer the curve than 2^-28 off by more. The alternatives: the core's pairs, far off within 2^-10; or triple pairs, three times the work. Recommended: as taken; triple pairs where a use meets the curve that closely. Taken.

S346. Where a part is infinite or NaN, each of the five takes the value of the C99 function it is a form of, as Annex G has none of its own: clog's for log2 and log10, the angle over ln b; clog's at 1 + z for log1p, and -inf with y's zero at z = -1; cexp's for exp2, y ln 2 for y; and cexp's less 1 for expm1, so that expm1(-inf + iy) is -1 and expm1(-0) -0. Each keeps conj(f(z)) = f(conj z), as Annex G's functions do.

  • Alternative: NaNs at every special part: values the limits give, such as log2(-inf) = inf + i pi / ln 2, lost.
  • Alternative: tables of their own: no standard to follow, and values that would differ from the identities each function's definition states.

Review. Taken: for, the limits' values, as Annex G's own functions give them, each the identity its definition states, checked entry by entry by t_cplx.c; against, none. The alternatives: NaNs, the limits' values lost; or tables with no standard. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S347. In the language, exp2, log2, log10, log1p and expm1 of a complex number, and log2 and log10 of a negative number and log1p of one below -1, are their principal values, math::complex<T>'s and so cplx.h's _c forms in the tower's type (S336), dense, as none of theirs at a Gaussian rational is one but a real value (S337); expr_context's real_results makes the latter a domain error (S340); and not_yet is step 23's third part's alone. log2(-8) is 3 + 4.532...im, its real part 3 exactly in long double.

  • Alternative: an exact real part beside a dense imaginary one, 3 exact in 3 + 4.532...im: a complex value of mixed exactness, which no other function gives, its sums with exact numbers exact in one part alone.

Review. Taken: for, chunk 41's rules as they stand, one answer with the face, the not_yet gates gone; against, none past chunk 41's. The alternative: a complex value of mixed exactness. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S348 to S356: chunk 43, 2026.11.02

The record: _handoffs/math98_chunk43_2026.11.02/README.md.

S348 to S356, chunk 43's, as decisions.html has them; each is reviewed there, S354 flagged, minor. The 17 outstanding decisions of S1 to S356 come first, by group and urgency (section 2.5); S258 and S322 are done. The text below is decisions_c43.md, which decisions_c43.py reads.

S348. A matrix keeps a complex element as it is, as S239 keeps a real one: where every element is a float, a double, a long double or a complex number of such parts, densely, in the widest one's type, as complex numbers -- math::complex, the C core's struct, row by row - where any element is complex, a real element x then held as x + 0i, as S273 rules; where any element is exact or boxed, an exact complex number among them, as values, each of its own kind. [1, 2im] is a matrix of values, exact; a matrix of exp(im) and sqrt(2) is one of complex long doubles, its second element read back as 1.414... + 0im. So that no element is rounded by being put in a matrix, and a dense complex matrix is the array cmat.h and LAPACK take.

  • Alternative: every complex matrix as values, each element a box of its own: no layout the C core takes, each operation boxing every element.
  • Alternative: a dense matrix of mixed real and complex elements, each kept real or complex: two arrays, or a tag per element, and no kernel takes either.

Review. Taken: for, no element rounded by being put in a matrix; a dense complex matrix the array cmat.h, LAPACK and the face's matrix<complex<T>> take, so that the algebra reaches the C core without a copy into another layout; an exact complex matrix exact; against, a real element of a dense complex matrix costs a zero part, and one exact complex element among dense ones makes the matrix one of values, each element boxed. The alternatives: every complex matrix as values, each operation boxing every element; a mixed dense layout no kernel takes. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S349. The arithmetic of matrices of complex numbers is the kind's, as S246 has it, a complex number among the scalars: A + B and A - B of one shape, -A, A z, z A and A / z, A B. Dense, it computes in the widest type of its operands, complex where either is, each element by math::complex's operators, so cplx.h's, a real operand's element or a real scalar taken as itself, as C99's G.5 and the language's numbers take it, so that a zero's sign survives and an infinite real part meets no 0 times infinity; a product's each element the sum from 0, in order, of its row's products with its column's, as the C++ face's kernels sum a math::complex matrix's. On values, each element by the value's own arithmetic, exact for exact elements. A + z, a matrix plus a number, stays a domain error, as for a real number (S246).

  • Alternative: a real operand widened to x + 0i first, as NumPy and MATLAB promote it: two arithmetics for one operation of a real and a complex number, the matrix's and the number's, and NaNs where 0 meets an infinity.

Review. Taken: for, one arithmetic for a complex number in a matrix and out of one, G.5's, so that A * 2 and each element times 2 agree bit for bit, a zero's sign and an infinity kept; a product's bits the face's kernels'; against, a product's sum in order, as every naive product, its error growing with n, where an optimized BLAS's blocked or fused sums give other bits. The alternative: a real operand widened first, as NumPy computes, NaNs where an infinity meets 0. Recommended: as taken; a compensated product where a use asks for one. Taken.

S350. A' is the conjugate transpose, as S273 rules, each complex element conjugated, a real one as it is, a dense complex element's zero part's sign flipped as cplx.h's conj flips it; transpose(A), a new function of the table, 52 now, is the transpose, and of a number the number itself. real, imag, conj and arg of a matrix are its elements', element by element, a matrix of the results kept as S348 keeps them, as MATLAB's, NumPy's and Julia's are; complex(A, B) of two matrices of one shape is the matrix of complex(a_ij, b_ij), and of a matrix and a number a domain error, as A + b is (S246). The C++ form is expr_transpose(A, false); expr_transpose(A) stays A'.

  • Alternative: complex(A, b) with b broadcast to every element, as MATLAB scales a scalar: a second rule of shapes beside S246's.
  • Alternative: abs of a matrix element by element too: |A| is a norm in the language's notation (S246), and norm the language's.

Review. Taken: for, MATLAB's and Julia's ' and transpose, and the parts of a matrix element by element as every system gives them; complex(A, B) shaped as A + B is (S246); against, one more name in the function table, 52, and A' other than NumPy's .T (S273). The alternatives: complex broadcasting a number, a second rule of shapes; abs element by element, where |A| is the language's norm. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S351. Linear algebra's functions take matrices of complex numbers: in the type of S257, the widest of the elements', of complex numbers where any element is one, an exact matrix exactly. Dense, det, inv and solve are cmat.h's - LU with partial pivoting by |re| + |im|, Gauss-Jordan and the elimination of [A | B], as LAPACK's zgetrf, zgetri and zgesv - bit for bit, a real operand of solve taken as x + 0i, cmat.h's arrays being complex; trace the kernels' sum from 0. norm is of the moduli: dense, the C core's scaled norm of the 2n parts, re and im in turn, which never overflows where the norm does not; on values, the root of the exact sum of re^2 + im^2, exact where it is a rational square. dot(a, b) is the sum from 0, in order, of conj(a_i) b_i, as S273 rules; cross conjugates nothing. Of numbers as scalars (S256): det, trace and eig z itself, inv 1 / z, a real 1 taken as itself, norm |z|, dot(a, b) conj(a) b, solve(a, b) b / a, in a complex type too.

  • Alternative: the face's generic kernels for dense det, inv and solve: the same bits (S315), cmat.h being the C core's, as decomp.h is the real matrices' path.
  • Alternative: dot bilinear, NumPy's dot: dot(a, a) then not norm(a)^2 (S273's own alternative).

Review. Taken: for, cmat.h's bits, the C core's, in every family; an exact matrix exact; the norm that never overflows where the norm does not; the conjugating dot S273 rules, dot(a, a) the norm squared; against, a real operand of solve taken as x + 0i where the arithmetic keeps it real (S349), so that a zero's sign can differ from A \ b computed by hand, and no condition estimate beside solve, as for real matrices (S257). The alternatives: the face's generic kernels, the same bits; a bilinear dot. Recommended: as taken; cond when a step asks, for both. Taken.

S352. eig of a matrix of complex numbers, and of a real one with a complex pair, gives every eigenvalue (S258, S273). Dense: a real matrix's, where each is real, as before, bit for bit; otherwise decomp.h's d_decomp_eigen's, LAPACK's dgeev's path, a pair exactly conjugate, complex throughout, a real eigenvalue's imaginary part +0; a Hermitian matrix's, A^H = A exactly, by cmat.h's cyclic Jacobi method with complex rotations, on A scaled exactly by a power of two, to its Frobenius norm times the type's epsilon, real; any other complex matrix's by cmat.h's d_cmat_eigen, zgeev's path, complex. An exact matrix's are found so in long double, each made exact where it is a Gaussian rational: d A's characteristic polynomial is monic over the Gaussian integers, d the least common multiple of A's parts' denominators, so each such eigenvalue's parts are multiples of 1/d, and the nearest such candidate of each eigenvalue found is tried exactly, divided out as often as it is a root. Every spectrum is ordered by real part, then by imaginary part, in value.h's exact order, as Julia sorts them; a spectrum found real, or rational throughout, ascends as before. expr_context's real_results makes a complex spectrum of a real matrix a domain error (S340). Each solver is given 30 QR steps a row in all, and at least the 1000 every matrix was given since chunk 36, which a real matrix of 700 rows ran out of, a domain error; one that converged within 1000 takes the same steps, so the same bits. eig([0, -1; 1, 0]) is [-im, im], exactly.

  • Alternative: a domain error for a complex spectrum, as until now: no eigenvalue of a rotation.
  • Alternative: S273's conjugate pair always side by side, the negative first, even where a real eigenvalue shares the pair's real part: a second key beside the parts, where the C core's and Julia's order is the parts'.

Review. Taken: for, every eigenvalue a matrix has, as LAPACK's and Julia's eigvals give them, a Hermitian matrix's real and accurate to its norm's epsilon, an exact matrix's exact wherever it is a Gaussian rational, one order for every spectrum, and a budget of steps that grows with the matrix, as LAPACK's does; against, an exact matrix's other algebraic eigenvalues are long doubles, a dense real matrix's repeated or nearly repeated eigenvalue can come as a complex pair with tiny imaginary parts, as floating-point QR finds it, and so a domain error under real_results, and no eigenvectors yet. The alternatives: a domain error for a complex spectrum; a second key for pairs. Recommended: as taken; eigenvectors when a step asks for eig's second output. Taken.

S358. quat.h's product, inverse and two quotients are sums of exact products by Ogita, Rump and Oishi's Dot2, each part rounded once: for float and double in double's pairs where double is evaluated in its own type, as SSE evaluates it, a float's products exact there and a double's Dekker's; for long double, and for float and double where double is evaluated wider, as on a 32-bit x87, in long double's pairs. Each part is as accurate as its sum computed in twice the pairs' precision and rounded once: within a step but where the sum cancels, by some 16 u^2 times its terms' magnitudes. The operands are scaled by powers of 2 where Dekker's products could overflow or lose their low parts; an operand with an infinite or NaN part takes the formula as written (_fast); an exact sum of products not all 0 is +0, as IEEE's arithmetic makes it, and a sum of zero products their own sum in order. Measured: 0 steps from the correctly rounded part at every reference but double's 8 at a sum cancelled to 2^-50 of its terms, within the promise; on the x86-64 here 61 ns a double product and 96 ns a float's, against 1.0 ns for multiply_fast and 431 ns for long double's.

  • Alternative: every family in long double's pairs: float's and double's exact sum rounded once nearly always, but some 430 ns a product, the x87's, where SSE's double pairs take a seventh of it.
  • Alternative: each family in its own type's pairs, as cplx.h's arithmetic: float's at twice float's precision, where double's pairs give float more for the same cost.
  • Alternative: an exact sum of each part, as an expansion: correctly rounded always, at several times Dot2's cost.

Review. Taken: for, each part of the product, the inverse and the quotients as accurate as twice its pairs' precision, as S276 rules, measured 0 steps from the correctly rounded part at every reference but where a sum cancels to 2^-50 of its terms; float's and double's at SSE's speed in double's pairs, a float's products exact; long double's, and the x87's, in long double's pairs; the formula's IEEE values for an infinity or a NaN; against, a product costs 60 to 100 ns for float and double and 430 ns for long double, against 1.0 ns for multiply_fast, some 60 to 100 times, where S276's review put it at three times the operations: without a fused multiply-add, which no constant expression has, each exact product is Dekker's 17 operations. The alternatives: every family in long double's pairs, exact but some 430 ns; each family in its own type's pairs, float's at twice float's precision; an exact sum, several times Dot2's cost. Recommended: as taken; the _fast forms for a renderer's inner loops. Taken.

S359. The inverse and the two quotients of S277: d_quat_*_inverse, q / |q|^2; divide(a, b), a b^-1, the quotient on the right; left_divide(a, b), a^-1 b, the quotient on the left; each part a numerator's pair over |q|^2's, as S358's. 0's inverse is a NaN in each part, the formula's 0 / 0 and Eigen's; an infinite q's the zeros of its conjugate's signs, as 1 / inf is 0; a NaN's NaNs. Where an operand is not finite or the divisor is 0, a quotient is a times b's inverse, the product as written.*

  • Alternative: 1 / 0 an infinity, as C99's Annex G makes a complex number's: a quaternion's infinity has no one direction to give it.
  • Alternative: the names rdivide and ldivide, MATLAB's: divide reads as C's /, which is the right quotient.

Review. Taken: for, the quotient on the right that S277 names /, and the left one, each part within a step; a zero's inverse NaN, as the formula and Eigen give it; an infinity's inverse 0, as 1 / inf is; against, no infinity for 1 / 0, where a complex number has Annex G's. The alternatives: 1 / 0 an infinity, with no one direction; MATLAB's names. Recommended: as taken. Taken. Corrected 2026.11.25 (chunk 65, S494): Eigen gives the zero quaternion, not NaNs; the NaN is the formula's and GLM's.

S360. The rotation is q v q^-1 for any q not 0: rotate, and to_matrix3 and to_matrix4, R / |q|^2, so that a q a few steps from unit length names the rotation of q / |q|, as v' = q v q does for a unit q (S276): each part from pairs of exact products in long double, over |q|^2's pair, within a step (measured 0 for float and double, 1 for long double). q = 0, or a part not finite, gives NaNs; v = 0 is left as it is. rotate_fast, to_matrix3_fast and to_matrix4_fast keep the former formulas, for a unit q: S276's "the rotation" taken to include its matrices, which a renderer uploads.* Some 900 ns a rotation, the kernel's long double, against 5 ns for the _fast form, as the faces' rotate is the accurate one.

  • Alternative: q v q* computed accurately: a q a few steps off unit length scales v by |q|^2, which q v q^-1 does not.
  • Alternative: the rotation and its matrices in double's pairs for float and double, as the product (S358): some seven times faster, at more code.

Review. Taken: for, the rotation that any q not 0 names, the rotation of q / |q|, each part within a step, so that rounding's few steps off unit length do not scale v; the former formulas kept as the _fast forms, the matrices among them, for a renderer; against, the accurate rotation costs some 900 ns in long double's pairs, against 5 ns for rotate_fast, and the faces' rotate is the accurate one; q = 0 gives NaNs where the former formula gave v. The alternatives: q v q* accurately, which a q off unit length scales; the rotation in double's pairs, as the product, seven times faster. Recommended: as taken, and the rotation and its matrices in double's pairs for float and double, as the product's (S358), at the next step that touches quat.h's kernels. S361. The norm and normalization: norm_c and norm, the scaled norm as a pair rounded once, +inf for an infinite part, NaN for a NaN, +0 for 0; normalize_c and normalize(out, q), each part over the norm's pair, q = 0 left as it is, an infinite part's quaternion the direction of its infinities, the limit of q / |q|, a NaN's NaNs. normalize takes no norm of the caller's now, as S276 puts the scaled norm in it; the _c and plain forms give the same bits, the root correctly rounded either way. normalize_fast is the reciprocal square root of the sum of squares, in the family's type and unscaled.   • Alternative: normalize(out, q, norm) as before: each caller's norm and its accuracy its own.

The alternative: the caller's norm, of the caller's accuracy. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S362. Construction: from_matrix3 and from_matrix4 by Shepperd's method, the largest of 4w^2, 4x^2, 4y^2 and 4z^2 - w first among equals, then x, y, z - giving its part, the others the off-diagonal sums and differences over 4 times it, then q or -q, whichever has w >= 0; a NaN or infinite entry gives NaNs; the result not normalized after, so that an orthogonal matrix's quaternion is within a step and a nearly orthogonal one's as near unit length as the matrix is orthogonal. from_two_vectors(a, b), the shortest rotation turning a's direction into b's: (a x b, |a| |b| + a . b) normalized, the sum taken as |a x b|^2 / (|a| |b| - a . b) where a . b < 0, so that it does not cancel; exactly opposed, the half turn about a x e_k, e_k the unit vector along a's smallest part (the first among equals); a zero vector, the identity. nlerp(a, b, t), (1 - t) a + t b along the shorter arc, normalized, exact at the ends before the root.

  • Alternative: Eigen's sign, w's as the branch leaves it: one rotation's quaternion either sign by which candidate was largest.
  • Alternative: from_matrix normalized: a step more for every exact rotation matrix.
  • Alternative: an opposed pair's axis from a fixed axis's cross product, as GLM's: none where a lies along that axis.

Review. Taken: for, Shepperd's method, the stable one, with one sign for every rotation; a nearly orthogonal matrix's quaternion as near unit length as the matrix is orthogonal, which normalizing would hide; two vectors' quaternion without cancellation where they nearly oppose, and an axis for exactly opposed ones whatever a's direction; nlerp along the shorter arc; against, from_matrix of a matrix far from orthogonal gives a quaternion far from unit length, which the caller normalizes. The alternatives: Eigen's sign; a normalized from_matrix; GLM's fixed axis. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

**S363. slerp along the shorter arc, b or -b: theta = 2 atan2(|a - b|, |a

  • b|), accurate near 0 where acos(a . b) is not; at theta = 0 the line (1 - t) a + t b; sin((1 - t) theta) / sin theta and sin(t theta) / sin theta otherwise, each part the two products' sum. a at t = 0, b or -b at t = 1, and a at every t between a and a, exactly. slerp_c is one long double kernel, each part a pair, rounded once; the plain slerp the same algorithm in the family's type on the C library's. Its parts are sums that may cancel, so it is measured against its largest part: slerp_c within a step of it, and 2 for long double (measured 0, 0 and 2), the plain one within 4 (measured 2).**
  • Alternative: the former acos(a . b), and a renormalized line past a . b = 0.9995: another curve near 0, and acos's error growing as 1 / theta there.

Review. Taken: for, the angle accurate near 0, so that slerp is one curve at every angle; its ends exact; a at every t between a and a; against, slerp's parts are bounded against its largest part, not each its own, as they are sums that may cancel. S364. exp, log, sqrt and real powers are the complex functions in q's plane (S275): f(q) = Re f(w + r i) + u Im f(w + r i), r = |v|, u = v / r; for v along im alone, y and z 0, cplx.h's function of w + x i itself, bit for bit, y and z as given, so that a complex number's value is the same as a quaternion's. In the _c kernels r is a pair and f corrected to first order for its low part; log and sqrt take q scaled by a power of 2 only outside the safe range, as near |q| = 1 the scale's k ln 2 would cancel log |q|; sqrt's vector part is v / (2 Re) where the real part is the larger, 2 Re Im = r being exact, and log's v / w where r < w 2^-(P/2 + 2). A part of v that is 0 stays 0, whatever it multiplies, an infinity or a NaN among them; a NaN part gives NaNs; an infinite part of v an infinite r along v's infinities. The _c forms are within a step for float and double and 4 for long double (measured 0, and 2 each), pow_c's of its largest part (measured 0, 1 and 3), as a whole power's parts, by cplx.h's repeated squaring, may cancel (S368); the plain forms, in the family's type with |v| rounded once, within 12, 4, 4 and 12 steps of the largest part at the references' |v| below 16 and |p| below 8 (measured 8, 3, 2 and 6), exp's and pow's error growing with |v| and p, as the cosine of a rounded |v| does. The faces take the _c forms (S365).

  • Alternative: the plain forms on the _c kernels' algorithm in long double on the C library's functions: as accurate as the _c forms, at long double's cost.
  • Alternative: q^p for an integer p by repeated products: exact where the products are, but a power with two definitions.

Review. Taken: for, the complex functions' principal values, a complex number's bit for bit, as S275 rules; the _c forms within a step for float and double, r carried as a pair so that a cosine of |v| near 0 is right; zero parts of v kept 0, so that the complex case's jm and km parts stay 0 whatever the plane's value; against, the plain forms take |v| rounded once, so that exp's and pow's errors, measured against the largest part, grow with |v| and with p: 8 and 6 steps at |v| below 16. The alternatives: the plain forms in long double on the C library's, as accurate as the _c forms at long double's cost; integer powers as products. Recommended: as taken for the _c forms, which the faces take; the plain forms carrying |v| as a pair, as the product's double pairs do, at the next step that touches quat.h's kernels. Flag: minor: the plain exp's and pow's error grows with |v| and p.

S365.   • Alternative: slerp, exp, log, sqrt and pow on the C library at run time, as from_axis_angle: faster, a few steps off and exp's growing with |v|.
  • Alternative: the generic path's slerp in T's arithmetic with its sines through double, as before: its own formula, a different curve.

The alternatives: the C library's at run time; the generic slerp's former formula. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S366. elementary.h's round_pair, which rounds a long double pair once to float or double, gives an infinity for a finite pair past the family's largest finite value, but for the midpoint above it with the sum below, which rounds to the largest value. It gave the largest finite value wherever the low part pointed back, as the midpoint test met infinity less infinity; the quaternions' references found it, a left quotient past DBL_MAX coming back DBL_MAX. Only values past a family's range change.

  • Alternative: each kernel testing for overflow before it rounds: the same test in every caller, and a caller that forgets it.

**S370. cplx.h's whole powers, z^n for n from -2^30 to 2^30, are carried in long double's pairs, as S368 recommended: repeated squaring from the exponent's highest bit, each product's parts sums of Dekker's exact products by power.h's pairs, the larger part rescaled into [1, 2) after each step and its binade kept apart, so that no step overflows or underflows, the binade clamped where the result is surely an infinity or 0; a negative n's power inverted at the end, as pairs, the conjugate over the squared modulus; each part then scaled and rounded once, into the subnormals as the exponential scales its own. The plain forms take the same kernel, bit for bit, as no C library has a faster whole power to the same bound. Each part is within a step of the correctly rounded part where it is at least |n| 2^(4

  • P) of the larger part, P long double's precision, and below that, where it may cancel past what the pairs carry, within |n| 2^(3 - 2P) of the larger part; a power exact in twice long double's precision comes exactly, and an exact zero part takes the sign d_cplx_ld_mul's and d_cplx_ld_div's exact products give. quat.h's pow_c, whose plane's power it is, is bounded of each part again: within a step for float and double and 4 for long double (measured 0, and 2).** Measured at the 1,027 points of math_tests_cplx_pow_refs.h: every part above its threshold within 0 steps in every family, and the 61 long double parts below it within 0.031 of their allowance; each whole power costs 461 to 1,690 ns a call from n = 2 to 100, in both forms and in double and float alike, where chunk 44's plain forms took 131 to 823 ns for double and 81 to 507 for float and its _c form 278 to 1,851: the plain forms 2.0 to 3.5 times as long for double and 3.3 to 6.2 for float, the _c form 0.9 to 1.7 times (raw/timing.txt).
  • Alternative: whole powers by exp(n log z), the angle carried in pairs: each part accurate, but a Gaussian integer's square no longer exact.
  • Alternative: the plain forms' whole powers squared in each family's own type, as chunk 42 made them, the _c form's alone in pairs: faster, but a part that cancels some steps of the larger part off in the plain forms, two bounds for one power.

Review. S371. A quaternion is a boxed kind of the value, expr/quaternion.hpp's, of rank 950: past the complex number's 900, so that in an operation of a quaternion and a real or complex number the quaternion's table handles it, and below the matrix's 1000, whose table handles a quaternion operand (S275, S238). It holds its parts as values where all four are exact, or where one is of a boxed kind that is not exact, and otherwise densely, as math::quaternion<T> of the widest floating-point type among them, the C core's array [x, y, z, w], each part its nearest there (S266). An exact quaternion whose jm and km parts come to 0 is the complex number of its other two, or the real number w where its im part is 0 too, as an exact complex number of imaginary part 0 is real (S327); an inexact one keeps its four parts, its zeros with their signs, as quat(x, 0, 0, 0) of a double x does. The kind's exact entry says which values are exact (S325), and its holders are counted atomically (S134): jm and km are a box each.

  • Alternative: dense always, in long double where the parts are exact: (1 + 2jm)(3 - km) no longer exactly 3 - 2im + 6jm - km.
  • Alternative: an inexact quaternion of jm and km parts 0 made complex too: a dense value changing kind with its parts' values, its zeros' signs lost, an operation's kind no longer its operands'.

The alternatives: dense always, no exact quaternion; an inexact one made complex, its zeros' signs lost. Recommended: as taken. Taken.

S372. A quaternion's arithmetic is the value's own, through the kind's table. Exact parts compute exactly by value.h's operations: Hamilton's product, im jm = km, of sixteen products, and the quotient p / q = p inv(q), the quotient on the right (S277), p q over |q|^2, an exact 0 divisor a pole; a part past the family becomes G2's long double, flagged. Otherwise an operation computes in the widest floating-point type of its operands, each exact part its nearest there, by quat.h's accurate forms through math::quaternion<T>: the product and the quotient each part rounded once from Dot2's sums (S358, S359), a sum each part rounded once. A real operand is taken as itself, as C99's G.5 takes one beside a complex number: a sum or a difference changes w alone, and a product or a quotient by it scales each part, so that a zero part's sign survives; a complex operand is taken with its im the first unit, a sum changing w and x alone; a real or complex number over a quaternion is the full quotient. No operator takes the quotient on the left: inv(q) p is written so, as S277 rules.*

  • Alternative: \ the quotient on the left, MATLAB's, as chunk 44's handoff had it: an operator S277 did not give, one more for the parser that S190's agreement is deciding.
  • Alternative: a real or complex operand taken as a quaternion of +0 parts: one path for every operand, a zero part's sign lost, as G.5 avoids for complex numbers.
  • Alternative: dense arithmetic by the _fast forms, the former formulas: some 60 times faster in double, each part within a few steps, not one.

**S373. |q| is the norm: of exact parts the root of the exact sum of their squares, exact where it is the square of a rational of the family, |1 + 2im

  • 2jm + 4km| being 5, and otherwise the long double nearest it; of dense parts quat.h's scaled norm, the root correctly rounded (S361). A quaternion equals a number whose parts equal its own, a real or complex number's missing parts 0, by value.h's exact order, and stands in no order otherwise, nor with a NaN part; the language's < <= > >= of one are a domain error. It converts to a float, double or long double where its im, jm and km parts are 0, of either sign, its w's nearest, with the rounding's direction; no exact quaternion converts, as none is real. Its text is w, then each other part's sign, magnitude and unit, 1 + 2im - 3jm + 4km, exact parts as integers or fractions, floating-point ones in max_digits10 digits, a part that is no finite number by its name, nan*jm; the language writes one in brackets, (1 + 2 * im - 3 * jm + 4 * km), and reads it back as its value.**
  • Alternative: abs the norm squared, as some libraries' norm is: no root to round, but not |q|, which the bars mean.
  • Alternative: an order of the parts taken in turn, for sorting: an order that no algebra of the quaternions keeps.

S374. The language's functions of a quaternion are those S275 names. quat(w, x, y, z) makes one of four real parts; parts(q) gives its parts as the row [w, x, y, z], of a real or complex number too, a missing part a 0 of its parts' kind; real and conj give its real part and conjugate, and q' its conjugate, as a complex number's z' is (S333); abs and the bars its norm; inv(q) its inverse, exact for exact parts; sign(q) q / |q|, exact where |q| is rational, a 0 itself; exp, log and sqrt the complex functions in q's plane, quat.h's _c forms in the tower's type, dense, sqrt exact where the root is a quaternion of rationals; and q ^ r of a real r exact by repeated squaring for a whole r, a negative one the inverse's, and for a rational p/s where q's principal s-th root is a quaternion of rationals, the Gaussian root in q's plane (S337) where |v| is rational, and otherwise quat.h's pow_c. S389. eig of a matrix of quaternions gives Zhang's standard right eigenvalues: the n complex numbers of imaginary part 0 or more, one from each class of similar right eigenvalues, as a 1 by n row, by real part and then imaginary. A triangular matrix's are its diagonal's, each element's own, w + |v| i, exactly where |v| is rational, since rounding would part a repeated pair. A Hermitian matrix's are real. S395 and S396: chunk 47, 2026.11.13

S395. A term is compiled once for a type, the first of the evaluation's fixes the owner chose on 2026.11.12 ("Speed, no API change"): expr_compile<T>(term), or expr_compile<T>(term, names), gives an expr_program<T>, in the new expr/program.hpp, for float, double or long double or the math::complex of one, and the handle's compile<T>() gives one too. Its steps are the typed fold's, in the order the fold computes, each operand before its operation, left to right: a literal rounded once to T, a constant's value in T, a variable's slot, an operator of the table's, a function's evaluator, indexing's NaN. A run does them on a stack whose greatest depth the compiler counted, in the caller's frame up to D_CFG_MATH_EXPR_PROGRAM_STACK (32), the language's first knob, in config/cpp/modules/math/expr/cfg_expr.h, which dconfig.h includes, and on the heap past it. A run's answer is the fold's, bit for bit, and its error the fold's first: the term's shape's when it is compiled, an unbound variable's at a run of bindings, the first read in the fold's order. A run takes T's own numbers in slot order, math::values, a vector of them or bindings, and a context, which each evaluator is given (S340). For it the complex fold's operations take their operands where they lie, in an array of the frame up to eight (expr_operands), where each built up to three vectors; the fold is faster with it, its answers unchanged. No constant is folded, and the value fold is not compiled. Measured (bench_program_c47.cpp, the median of seven timings, five terms of 8 to 40 steps): a run of doubles takes 2.9 to 11.4 times the same term written in C++, where the typed fold takes 79 to 624 times, so a run is 26 to 55 times sooner than a fold; a run of bindings, which looks each name up, takes 1.5 to 3.1 times a run of doubles; a run in math::complex<double> is 8.1 to 15.2 times sooner than that fold; a compile, 992 to 5,874 ns, pays for itself at the second run.

  • Alternative: constants folded when the term is compiled, 2 * pi one step of 2 * pi * x: fewer steps, and a folded constant computed under the compiler's rounding mode and context, not the run's, so that a run would no longer be the fold wherever either differs. A literal is rounded from its text in any mode (decimal.h), so literals are made at compile time.
  • Alternative: machine code, through LLVM as SymEngine's lambdify does, or a JIT: several times faster again on a long term; a dependency the framework has no other like, code generated for each platform, and none at all in a constant expression.
  • Alternative: a tree of nodes specialized to each operation, ExprTk's design: a call through a pointer at every node, which the flat steps already make, and a node allocated for each.
  • Alternative: the value fold compiled too: a value's arithmetic allocates and its errors are values, so a program of values would save the lookups alone; S396 makes the value's arithmetic itself faster instead.
  • Alternative: a run of an array of math::complex<T>: a complex number cannot say it is real, and the fold's answers turn on it (S339), so a run in a complex type takes math::values or bindings.

Review. Taken: for, the 130 times a fold cost, as the evaluation measured it, cut to a few times the term written in C++, with nothing given up: each run's answer the fold's bit for bit and its error the fold's first, in all six types the fold takes, proved on 2,000 random terms in each against the fold itself and on 500,000 points of five terms by the benchmark; a compile pays for itself at the second run; a run of a real type allocates nothing, nor, with the complex fold's operations in place, a run of a complex one; the API only grows, as the owner chose. Against: a second way to evaluate a term in a type, eval once and compile for many, which a reader must learn; a run of bindings looks each name up, several times a run of numbers; past 32 levels of operands a run allocates; still no machine code, several times slower than SymEngine's LLVM back end would be on a long term; the names expr_compile, expr_program and expr_variables are prefixed, and will move with math::expr::. The alternatives: constants folded; machine code; a tree of specialized nodes; the value fold compiled; a run of math::complex<T>s. Recommended: as taken.

S396. The value's binary arithmetic, the second of the speed fixes, sends two inline numbers to their arithmetic before the checks that cannot hold for them: two integers whose sum, difference or product a test of the operands shows fits the word, at once, a product where each factor's magnitude is below 2^(w/2 - 1); two exact numbers to the exact arithmetic; two numbers of one inexact kind to that kind's arithmetic, past the checks for errors, boxes, booleans and none. The floating-point tail is one helper, which both paths call. math::value's move assignment moves the value once, where it moved it into a temporary and swapped. Each result is the general path's, field by field. value_fast_c47.sh compares chunk 46's value.h with chunk 47's on 2,214,884 pairs in each of eight builds, and none differs; the two faults planted in the shortcut, a product bound too large and flags dropped, it catches. t_value.c gains the operands at 2^(w/2 - 1) and three flagged values, each checked against its exact reference. Measured (bench_value_c47.sh, chunk 46's header and chunk 47's built alike, the median of three rounds): math::value's sum of two doubles from 39.2 to 13.0 ns, 75 times a double's sum to 25; a product from 39.0 to 13.3; a sum of two integers from 28.7 to 21.0; of two rationals, which no shortcut takes, from 60.9 to 57.8.

  • Alternative: every integer product that fits taken at once, by the two words of d_internal_rational_mul_wide: two words multiplied for each product, where most factors are small; a product of large factors that fits takes the exact arithmetic, as before.

Review. Taken: for, math::value's arithmetic on the numbers it most often holds several times faster, in C and C++ alike, with every result the general path's field by field, proved by value_fast_c47.sh against chunk 46's header on two million pairs in eight builds, and by t_value.c against its exact reference with operands at the shortcut's bound; the faults a shortcut invites, a bound too large and flags lost, caught by the comparison. Against: three paths to read where one was, though each ends in the function the general path calls; an integer product of large factors that fits still takes the long way; the value stays 64 bytes, so copies still cost what they did. The alternatives: the shortcut in the C++ face alone; a union; every product that fits by a wide multiply. Recommended: as taken.
